L31: ## I. Executive Summary
L32: ## 1. Overview
L33: ## 1.1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L34: IntraBio Ltd. (Applicant) submitted this new drug application (NDA) for levacetylleucine, an N-acetyl-L-leucine drug product, also referred to by the company development code IB1001 (tradename Aqneursa), seeking approval of this small molecule as a treatment of Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC).
L35: Niemann-Pick disease type C is a rare and serious neurodegenerative lysosomal disorder caused by defective intracellular lipid trafficking and metabolism. As a result, patients with NPC have progressive accumulation of several lipid species in affected cells in the nervous system and several visceral organs. NPC is neurodegenerative, most commonly impacting ambulation, fine motor skills, speech, swallowing, and cognition in addition to visceral symptoms. After the aforementioned neurological symptoms, ataxia is one of the most common neurological symptoms reported. This NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team. Each discipline recommends approval of levacetylleucine for the treatment of the neurological manifestations of NPC in adults and pediatric patients weighing at least 15 kg. The signatory authority for this application concurs and recommends approval for this indication.
L36: Substantial evidence of effectiveness of levacetylleucine for NPC was established using data from one adequate and well-controlled trial and confirmatory evidence (CE). For full details, refer to Section 1.2.
L37: The available clinical safety data show that levacetylleucine is safe for its intended use. Common adverse reactions include dysphagia, upper respiratory tract infections, abdominal pain and vomiting. Aggravation of pre-existing rosacea was also reported as possibly related to treatment during the trial. These risks observed are deemed acceptable with appropriate product labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
L38: Data from dose-ranging nonclinical embryofetal development studies indicates that levacetylleucine is fetotoxic (decrease in fetal weight, increase in resorptions, and post-implantation loss) and causes external and skeletal malformation in both rodents and non-rodents. The embryo-fetal risks are adequately communicated through labeling. As described in Section 2.2, we conclude that the improvement in neurological function observed with use of levacetylleucine outweighs the risks when levacetylleucine is used as recommended in the approved labeling.
L39: ## 1.2. Conclusions on Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
L40: Substantial evidence of effectiveness (SEE) of levacetylleucine for NPC was established using data from one adequate and well-controlled trial and confirmatory evidence. The adequate and
L41: well-controlled trial, Trial IB1001-301, demonstrated a clinically meaningful improvement in neurological function that reached numerical statistical significance in children and adults with NPC aged 5 years and older. With a p-value of &lt;0.005 and a clinically meaningful magnitude of improvement demonstrated for the primary endpoint in the treatment arm compared to placebo, the review team considered the results of this trial highly persuasive.
L42: CE is comprised of both clinical data and nonclinical mechanistic data that support the results of Trial IB1001-301. The clinical data include the results of an open-label study of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC, Trial IB1001-201. Thirty-three patients with NPC were enrolled in the trial, which consisted of a 2-week baseline period, a 6-week treatment period, and a 6-week washout period. The rater-blinded primary endpoint, clinical impression of change in severity (CI-CS), measured each subject's change in performance on their primary anchor test (a timed subtest of the spinocerebellar ataxia functional index, either the 8-Meter Walk Test or the 9-Hole Peg Test of the dominant hand) between the beginning and end of the treatment and washout periods. Improvement in the CI-CS was observed in the treatment period and deterioration during the washout period. While Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label study, the methodology of blinding the CI-CS raters to time and treatment assignment increases the interpretability of the resulting scores by reducing the influence of knowledge of treatment assignment. The CI-CS results are supported by the timed spinocerebellar ataxia functional index subtests and indicate either improvement or stability in the treatment period, and either deterioration or stability in the washout period. Consequently, the Agency concludes that Trial IB1001-201 provided sufficient supportive evidence that the subjects' performance on the selected measures of gait and fine motor function is comparatively favorable in the treatment period.
L43: The supportive nonclinical mechanistic data that contributed to CE include in vitro and in vivo data in (1) wildtype and NPC1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) (*Npc1*-/- CHO) cells; (2) fibroblasts derived from patients with NPC; and (3) *Npc1*-/- mice, all of which support the potential for levacetylleucine treatment to slow disease progression in patients with NPC. Published data suggest that treatment of *Npc1*-/- CHO cells with levacetylleucine may modestly reduce accumulation of sphingosine, glycosphingolipids (GSLs), and cholesterol and may reduce lysosomal volume compared to untreated *Npc1*-/- CHO cells. Additionally, in vitro treatment of fibroblasts from patients with NPC with severe clinical presentation suggest that levacetylleucine may reduce lysosomal volume in these cells. Published data from studies in NPC1-null mice (*Npc1*-/- mice) treated with levacetylleucine reported a slight reduction in GSLs in brain and cerebellum; improvements in ataxia, gait, and mobility; reduction in neuroinflammation in the cerebellum and a small increase in lifespan compared to untreated *Npc1*-/- mice. These published nonclinical studies suggest that amelioration of biochemical (sphingosine, GSLs and cholesterol), cellular (lysosomal volume, neuroinflammation) and functional (ataxia, gait) changes in nonclinical NPC models (in vitro and in vivo) upon treatment with levacetylleucine may provide plausible mechanistic support for the effectiveness of levacetylleucine when administered to patients with NPC. The demonstration of improvement in neurological function (ataxia, gait, stance, sitting, and speech) in subjects with NPC in Trial IB1001-201, in combination with the supportive nonclinical mechanistic data derived from published literature, constitute the CE for this application. Refer to Section 6.3.3 for details.
L44: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L45: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L46: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L47: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC) is a rare, autosomal recessive neurodegenerative disorder caused by mutations in the genes NPC1 or NPC2. Impaired NPC protein function results in impaired intracellular trafficking and metabolism, causing accumulation of unesterified cholesterol and glycosphingolipids.
The primary manifestations and rate of disease progression is heterogeneous. NPC can present with severe hepatic failure and/or respiratory failure in infancy up to the age of 2 years or with primarily progressive neurological degeneration including cognitive impairment, gross and fine motor delays, ataxia, psychiatric symptoms, vertical supranuclear palsy, dystonia, and epilepsy.
Infants with systemic disease usually die in infancy. Across all types, the median age of death is 13 years (range 0.1 to 69 years). Death is most commonly due to respiratory failure in the setting of infection and/or aspiration. While patients who are diagnosed during adulthood may survive longer, they become fully dependent for supportive measures/ADLs
NPC is a serious and life-limiting neurodegenerative disease that affects both children and adults and leads to premature death.
Current treatment options	Only very recently has one therapy been approved in the United States for the treatment of NPC. The current standard of care is primarily supportive management of individual disease manifestations. Zavesca (miglustat) is approved in the European Union, Canada, and Japan for the treatment of progressive neurological manifestations in adult and pediatric patients with NPC. In the United States, miglustat is approved for other indications including Gaucher disease. Off-label use is considered standard of care in adult and pediatric patients with NPC. Despite widespread use of miglustat, there is continued disease progression, thus there is an unmet need for approved therapies.
While there is one very recently approved drug to treat the neurological manifestations of NPC, there remains a need for additional therapeutic options for patients diagnosed with NPC, a devastating disease. Because concomitant miglustat use is common for patients with NPC, the impact of this concomitant exposure on efficacy and safety was considered during this review.
L48: ## NDA 219132
## Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L49: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Aqneursa (levacetylleucine, IB1001) is a modified amino acid administered orally and intended for the treatment of the neurological symptoms of NPC.
The efficacy of levacetylleucine was evaluated in an adequate and well-controlled trial (Trial IB1001-301) in children and adults with NPC. Changes in neurological symptoms as measured by a functional Scale for Assessment and Rating of Ataxia (fSARA) assessment of gait, stance, sitting, and speech were compared in subjects randomly assigned to treatment sequences to receive levacetylleucine followed by placebo or receive placebo followed by levacetylleucine. Most of the subjects (85%) in the trial used miglustat at baseline.
The estimated mean fSARA score was 5.1 when subjects were treated with Aqneursa and 5.6 when subjects were treated with placebo. The estimated treatment difference in mean fSARA scores was -0.45 (95% confidence interval [CI]: -0.70, -0.19), favoring levacetylleucine and meeting numerical statistical significance (p=0.0009). This level of change on the fSARA is expected to affect how patients with NPC feel and function. The subgroup analyses showed a consistent treatment difference, including for the subgroup of subjects not on background miglustat.
Confirmatory evidence (CE) of effectiveness is derived from the phase 2 open-label trial with a rater blinded primary endpoint, IB1001-201, which showed improvement in subject's performance on either 8MWT or 9HPT-D during the treatment period, and deterioration during the washout period (Hodges-Lehmann estimate: 1.0, 90% CI: 0.25, 1.75).
Additional supportive evidence is derived from published mechanistic studies showing that levacetylleucine may reduce accumulation of lipids (sphingosine, glycosphingolipids, cholesterol) and reduce lysosomal volume in Npc1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells in vitro. In patient-derived cells, levacetylleucine was reported to reduce lysosomal volume. In Npc1+/− mice, levacetylleucine was reported to slightly reduce GSLs in brain and cerebellum and to reduce ataxia, leading to improved gait and mobility. Levacetylleucine was also reported to reduce neuroinflammation in cerebellum and slightly improve lifespan. The published data described above are supportive of the potential for levacetylleucine to reduce aspects of NPC pathophysiology and is consistent with results from clinical trials.
The observed treatment difference is clinically meaningful to patients with NPC and/or their caregivers.
The results of the adequate and well-controlled trial were highly persuasive. These results, in combination with the additional clinical and nonclinical data that both support CE, are adequate to support a treatment benefit. The data support the use of levacetylleucine alone or with background miglustat.
L50: ## NDA 219132
## Aqeursa (levacetylleucine)
L51: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	The high rate of concomitant miglustat use limits the interpretability of the safety data in determining the risk of use of levacetylleucine alone. There is clinical overlap of the adverse events identified during Trial IB1001-301 with those identified in miglustat labeling.
The most noteworthy adverse reactions occurring in at least 5% of subjects in Trial IB1001-301 were abdominal pain, dysphagia, upper respiratory tract infections, and vomiting, and were more common in subjects treated with levacetylleucine than placebo.
Subjects who are pregnant or lactating were not evaluated in the development program.
Patient risk for adverse events with long-term exposure beyond two years is currently unknown.
Safety concerns from nonclinical studies included embryo-fetal toxicities in rats and rabbits (decrease in fetal weight, increase in resorptions and post-implantation loss, and external and skeletal malformations).
Juvenile toxicity, fertility and early embryonic development, pre- and postnatal development, and carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted pre-approval.
Carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted.
Levacetylleucine's risks can be adequately mitigated with labeling and routine postmarketing surveillance.
We are requiring juvenile toxicity, fertility, and early embryonic development, pre- and postnatal development, and carcinogenicity studies to be conducted postapproval.
L52: Abbreviations: 8MWT = 8-meter walk test; 9HPT-D = 9-Hole Peg Test of the dominant hand; ADL = activity of daily living; CE = confirmatory evidence; CI = confidence interval; CHO = Chinese hamster ovary; fSARA = functional Scale for Assessment and Rating of Ataxia; GSL = glycosphingolipid; NPC = Niemann-Pick disease type C
L53: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L54: NPC is a rare and progressive neurovisceral disorder caused by mutations in the genes *NPC1* or *NPC2* (Patterson 1993). These mutations result in impaired intracellular trafficking and accumulation of unesterified cholesterol and GSLs within the late endosomes and lysosomes of affected cells in the brain and other tissues. NPC is always progressive, but the primary manifestations, severity, and rate of progression are variable. Individuals who develop the symptoms of NPC in early childhood (after the age of 2 years) have a primarily neurodegenerative course with progressive motor and cognitive impairment, supranuclear gaze palsy, dysphagia, dysarthria, ataxia, and epilepsy, ultimately progressing to respiratory failure and death.
L55: There is currently only one very recently approved treatment for NPC. The current standard of care is primarily supportive, however the substrate reducing therapy Zavesca (miglustat) is approved in the European Union, Canada, and Japan for the treatment of NPC and off-label use is considered standard of care in the United States. Despite widespread use of miglustat, NPC continues to be progressive and life-limiting. Therefore, treatment and cure of NPC continue to represent unmet needs.
L56: The benefit of levacetylleucine is based on comparison of functional Scale for Assessment and Rating of Ataxia scores in 60 subjects aged 5 to 67 years with NPC and at least one neurological symptom randomized either to levacetylleucine or placebo for 12 weeks. The estimated treatment difference in mean functional Scale for Assessment and Rating of Ataxia scores at the end of week 12 was -0.45 (95% confidence interval: -0.70, -0.19), favoring the levacetylleucine arm and meeting numerical statistical significance (p&lt;0.001). CE of effectiveness is derived from a separate phase 2 rater blinded open-label trial (Trial IB1001-201) and nonclinical pharmacology studies (in vitro and in vivo) which are considered supportive of the overall package of CE.
L57: While no subjects under 5 years old were enrolled, extrapolation of efficacy to children who are under 5 years old using pharmacokinetic data are supported by the similar disease pathophysiology and predominant clinical manifestations in children with NPC with disease onset after infancy. Similarly, a limited amount of extrapolation of clinical safety to children under 5 years old and weight at least 15 kg was reasonable given the favorable benefit/risk profile, the similar disease pathophysiology across the age spectrum, the ability to utilize pharmacokinetic data for dose selection, and the lack of anticipated differential safety risk.
L58: While there is a limited amount of safety data in younger children, the available data support that levacetylleucine is safe for its intended use in adults and pediatric patients \ge 15 kg and the overall benefit/risk is favorable. Common adverse reactions include dysphagia, abdominal pain, and vomiting. Aggravation of pre-existing rosacea was also reported as possibly related to treatment during the trial. These risks can be adequately mitigated via product labeling and further evaluated during routine pharmacovigilance.
L59: Safety concerns from nonclinical studies include embryo-fetal toxicity. Uncertainties include the lack of juvenile toxicity studies, fertility studies, and embryofetal, prenatal and postnatal development studies, and carcinogenicity studies. These studies will be conducted as postmarketing requirements.
L60: ## NDA 219132
## Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L61: Given that NPC is a serious and life-limiting rare disease with only one recently approved drug to treat the neurological manifestations of NPC and a remaining unmet need for additional therapeutic options, the review team has determined the benefit-risk of levacetylleucine to be favorable for the treatment of adults and pediatric patients weighing at least 15 kg.
L62: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L63: ## 3. Introduction
L64: Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC) is an autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder caused by mutations in the genes *NPC1* (95%) or *NPC2* (5%) (Patterson 1993). NPC is a rare disease that has an estimated prevalence of one to three cases per million in the United States (US), although this is thought to be an underestimate given the heterogeneity of this disease and likelihood of missed diagnoses in patients with milder phenotypes. Estimated incidences in different populations around the world have ranged from 1:90,000 to 1:120,000 live births (Wassif et al. 2016) (Labrecque et al. 2021).
L65: NPC1 and NPC2 are important for late endosomal and lysosomal lipid transport and homeostasis. Their loss of function impedes cellular lipid trafficking and causes the accumulation of unesterified cholesterol, glucosylceramide, and gangliosides in the late endosomes and lysosomes of affected cells (Tang et al. 2010). The accumulation of cholesterol and other lipid species in peripheral tissues (mainly the liver and spleen) and in neurons and other non-neuronal cells in the central nervous system is thought to ultimately lead to the devastating and progressive neurovisceral symptoms that are the hallmarks of NPC via downstream processes that are not fully understood.
L66: NPC is always progressive, but the primary manifestations and prognosis are heterogeneous, ranging from a rapidly progressive neonatal form to an adult-onset form that is characterized by a chronic neurodegenerative disease course. NPC is typically categorized into age-dependent phenotypes, recognizing that they likely represent a continuum rather than distinct phenotypes. The four phenotypes described are 1) early infantile, with perinatal onset to the age of 2 years, (2) late infantile, with onset between the ages of 2 and 6 years, (3) juvenile, with onset between the ages of 6 and 15 years, and (4) adolescent/adult, with onset after the age of 15 years. The early infantile presentation is predominantly visceral, with hepatosplenomegaly, jaundice, and pulmonary disease, although some also have neurological symptoms (Seker Yilmaz et al. 2020). Individuals with early infantile onset tend to have a rapidly progressive disease course, progressing to death in early childhood. The late infantile and juvenile presentations are predominantly neurological with progressive decline in previously attained cognitive, gross, and fine motor skills as well as progressive dysarthria and dysphagia. Additional neurological symptoms include ataxia, cataplexy, vertical supranuclear gaze palsy, and epilepsy. Adolescent and adult onset of disease can be more insidious, with cognitive and behavioral changes or other psychiatric manifestations at the onset, followed by chronic neurological decline (Las Heras et al. 2023).
L67: Disease progression most commonly impacts ambulation, fine motor skills, speech, swallowing, and cognition, ultimately progressing to respiratory failure and death. Progressive neurological symptoms also include ataxia and other movement disorder symptoms and epilepsy. Ataxia is the most common movement disorder that occurs in NPC and can sometimes be a presenting symptom of the disease (Devaraj et al. 2022) (Anheim et al. 2014). While lifespan can vary from a few days (severe neonatal onset) to a few decades, the reported median age of death is 13 years (range: 0.1 to 69 years) (Bianconi et al. 2019).
L68: An Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting was held on March 18, 2019 and was hosted by 8 patient advocacy organizations for NPC.1 During the meeting, patients and caregivers shared their perspectives on disease symptoms, the impact of NPC on their daily lives, current experience with treatment, and expectations for future treatments. Eighty-three patients and caregivers responded to a survey prior to the meeting; the age of onset of neurological symptoms (developmental delays, ataxia, vertical gaze palsy, etc.) for most patients was after the age of 2 years (36.7% were 2 to 5 years old, 27.8% were 6 to 15 years old, and 12.7% were 16 to 29 years old). The six symptoms reported as having the greatest impact on patient and caregivers' daily lives were ambulation/walking difficulties and cognitive impairment, followed by swallowing difficulty, speech problems, seizures, and fine motor skills. Ambulation/walking and fine motor skills are often significantly impacted by ataxia in NPC; for example, one father of a 16-year-old male who had the onset of impaired coordination at 8 years old said “Less than one year after ataxia first appeared, his condition had deteriorated markedly. His gait became staggered, his handwriting reverted to kindergarten level, and the ataxia became more pronounced. Simple activities such as brushing teeth, or shampooing hair, became difficult tasks.” The most meaningful targets for drug treatment were summarized as impacting neurological disease symptoms, including any slowing, halting, or improvement of disease symptoms.
L69: In the United States, there is only one recently approved treatment for the neurological manifestations of NPC; additional therapeutic options for this devastating disease are needed. The current standard of care is primarily supportive and includes the treatment of epilepsy with anti-seizure medications as well as interventions to support feeding, mobility, communication, and behavior. Miglustat is an iminosugar that acts as a competitive and reversible inhibitor of the enzyme glucosylceramide synthase, the initial enzyme in the synthesis of many glycosphingolipids (GSLs). Miglustat was approved in the United States in 2003 for patients with mild or moderate Gaucher disease when enzyme replacement therapy is not a therapeutic option. Miglustat was also approved for the treatment of the progressive neurological manifestations of NPC in pediatric and adult patients in many countries outside the United States. Miglustat is often prescribed “off-label” for patients with NPC in the United States and is considered standard of care for NPC amongst clinical experts.
L70: Patients with NPC have a substantial and unmet treatment need. While miglustat has been reported in the medical literature to potentially slow disease progression in some patients, NPC remains progressive and life-limiting in all affected individuals. In the Voice of the Patient report for NPC (Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development meeting) (EL-PFDD 2019), this unmet need was summarized as follows: “Patients and families most desire new treatments for neurological symptoms and improved quality of life even if they do not extend life span. Recognizing the heterogeneity of disease progression and symptoms, patients with NPC and their families emphasize the need for multiple therapeutic options so that treatment approaches can be tailored to meet individual patient needs.”
L71: N-acetyl-L-leucine (levacetylleucine) is a modified amino acid and is the L-enantiomer of N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The racemate N-acetyl-DL-leucine has been available in France since 1957
L72: 1 Externally Lead Patient-Focused Drug Development Meeting on March 18, 2019 was hosted by: Ara Parseghian Medical Research Fund at Notre Dame, Hide &amp; Seek Foundation, Dana's Angels Research Trust, Hope for Marian, National Niemann-Pick Disease Foundation, Niemann-Pick Canada, Firefly Fund, and Johnathon's Dreams.
L73: as a short-term oral treatment in tablet form primarily used for acute vertigo in adults. The Applicant states that levacetylleucine reduced unesterified cholesterol in fibroblasts derived from patients with NPC. In addition, the Applicant cited published data in which *Npc1-/-* mice treated with levacetylleucine showed a slight reduction in a subset of GSLs in the brain (GM2, GM1a) and a small reduction in GA1 and GA2 in the cerebellum (Kaya et al. 2021). The magnitudes of these effects were sufficiently small that it is unclear if they were physiologically meaningful; however, there was an apparent reduction in CD68 staining in *Npc1-/-* mice that were treated with levacetylleucine compared with that of untreated *Npc1-/-* mice. These changes were accompanied by histological evidence of reduced CD68 in the cerebellum in levacetylleucine-treated animals compared with untreated *Npc1-/-* mice. In addition, some markers of motor improvement were observed; however, the magnitude of the effect was relatively small. These effects were accompanied by a small (8-day) increase in median survival.

L90: ## 3.2. Approach to the Clinical Review
L91: The Applicant submitted data from two trials that enrolled patients with NPC and one trial that enrolled patients with a different lysosomal storage disorder, GM2 gangliosidosis. The benefit-risk assessment is based on the single adequate and well-controlled trial in subjects with NPC (Trial IB1001-301) with supportive evidence from an open-label rater-blinded study (Trial IB1001-201) in subjects with NPC. The trial in GM2 gangliosidosis was reviewed for safety signals and trial withdrawals or deaths only and was not included in our efficacy assessment.
L92: The clinical trials conducted to support the benefit-risk assessment of levacetylleucine for the treatment of the neurological manifestations of NPC are summarized in Table 3.
L93: ## Determination of Efficacy
L94: To evaluate the efficacy of levacetylleucine for treating the neurological symptoms of NPC, the results of the randomized, double-blinded period of phase 3 clinical trial IB1001-301 was considered the primary source of efficacy data. A total of 60 subjects who received both levacetylleucine and placebo in Trial IB1001-301 were evaluated. At the time of NDA submission on January 24, 2024, an open-label extension (OLE) phase was ongoing, and the efficacy data were not submitted. In addition to Trial IB1001-301, the open-label phase 2 clinical trial IB1001-201 provided confirmatory evidence (CE).
L95: ## Determination of Safety
L96: The safety assessment was primarily based on data from Trial IB1001-301, the only source of randomized, placebo-controlled data in subjects with NPC. The safety data from the open-label study of levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC, Trial IB1001-201, as well as available data from the OLEs of both Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201 were also reviewed separately. The NPC safety population consisted of 84 subjects who received levacetylleucine, including 28 pediatric subjects and 56 adult subjects. Lastly, safety data from Trial IB1001-202, an open-label study of levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis, was also reviewed for potential safety signals.
L97: The analysis of general safety assessment of adverse events (AEs), laboratory evaluations, vital signs, and electrocardiograms was based on descriptive summaries and review of source data. Case report forms and subject narratives were also reviewed for premature trial discontinuations or withdrawals. Clinical trial data were independently analyzed using JMP and Python software. All safety assessments and conclusions are those of the clinical review team unless otherwise specified.

L124: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L125: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L126: NPC is a lysosomal storage disease that is biochemically characterized by increased storage of multiple lipids including low-density lipoprotein-derived cholesterol, sphingoid bases (e.g., sphingosine and sphinganine), and other sphingolipids including GSLs and sphingomyelin. The combined biochemical and cellular defects in NPC lead to widespread pathology in the brain including neurodegeneration and neuroinflammation, and to the clinical signs of NPC. The pharmacological effects of levacetylleucine (also referred to as N-acetyl-L-leucine, NALL, or IB1001) in treating NPC are discussed here. It is concluded that the pharmacology parameters evaluated in nonclinical models are primarily proof-of-concept that suggest plausible mechanistic support for the overall package of CE of effectiveness for levacetylleucine in patients with NPC.
L127: In their submission, the Applicant cited published data that describe potential modes of action whereby treatment with levacetylleucine slows the decline of biochemical and cellular defects present in nonclinical models of NPC, both in vitro and in vivo. These publications suggest that treatment with levacetylleucine may reduce accumulation of sphingosine, GSLs, and cholesterol, and that this treatment reduced lysosomal volume in NPC1-deficient Chinese hamster ovary cells in vitro. In vitro levacetylleucine treatment was also found to reduce lysosomal volume in cultured fibroblasts obtained from patients with NPC.
L128: In vivo studies in *Npc1-/-* mice, a nonclinical model of NPC, were conducted in which animals were treated pre-symptomatically (i.e., from the age of 3 weeks). The authors reported a reduction in total GSL levels in the forebrain (GM1a and GM2) and cerebellum (GA2 and GA1). The authors also reported a reduction in ataxic symptoms in *Npc1-/-* mice treated pre-symptomatically at a later age (8 weeks old). When initiated at the age of 3 weeks, treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest increase in survival and a reduction in markers of neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum.
L129: These findings suggest that levacetylleucine may slow disease progression in mice by an undefined mechanism; however, these findings do not provide direct support for the primary efficacy endpoint measured in clinical trials (Trial IB1001-201, Trial IB1001-301, or both). As such, the nonclinical data can only suggest plausible mechanistic CE for levacetylleucine in treating patients with NPC and should only be considered as a contributing factor in conjunction with strong evidence of clinical efficacy in the phase 3 clinical trial, Trial IB1001-301, conducted in patients with NPC.
L130: For additional details of cited publication see Section 13.2.2.
L131: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L132: The pharmacologic activity, pharmacokinetics (PK), and clinical pharmacology of levacetylleucine that are relevant to the interpretation of benefit and risk are summarized in Table 5.
L133: **Table 5. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L134: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Mechanism of action	The mechanism of action of levacetylleucine is unknown.
Active moieties	N-acetyl-L-leucine (NALL) is the active moiety. After being absorbed, NALL is metabolized into acetate and L-leucine by ubiquitously expressed enzymes. No other active metabolite of NALL has been identified.
QT prolongation	The Applicant has not conducted a thorough QT/QTc study to assess the effect of NALL on QT interval prolongation. According to the applicant's evaluation, the nonclinical and clinical data demonstrated no potential for QT/QTc prolongation and no other cardiovascular related findings. The review team agreed with the applicant's evaluation and agreed with waiving a TQT study. See Cardiac IRT team review in DARRTS dated January 26, 2024 (Reference ID: 5317889)
General Information
Bioanalysis	Levacetylleucine concentrations in human plasma were quantified using a validated LC-MS/MS assay. The sensitivity of the assay was 100 ng/mL.
Healthy subjects versus patients	The pharmacokinetics (PK) of NALL has not been assessed in healthy subjects.
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen (or single dose, if more relevant for the drug)	The full PK profile of NALL was assessed at steady state in 17 subjects with NPC after 365 days (\pm14 days) of dosing in the long-term extension phase of a phase 2 study. NALL maximum concentration (C_{max}) and area under the curve from time 0 to 24 hours (AUC_{0-24h}) were 8.3 (3.3) \mug/mL and 33.2 (12.5) h\cdot\mug/mL. No sex differences in PK parameters were observed.
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	The maximum proposed daily dose is 4 grams taken as 2 grams in the morning, 1 gram at noon, and 1 gram in the evening. This dose is the maximum daily dose tested in the clinical trials.
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	A maximum tolerated dose has not been determined
Dose proportionality	Dose proportionality was not assessed in the submitted clinical trials.
Accumulation	No accumulation was observed after repeat administration
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial formulations	The phase 3 trial used the to-be-marketed formulation; therefore, a PK bridging study between the to-be-marketed formulation and clinical trial formulation is not needed.
L135: ## NDA 219132
## Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L136: Characteristic	Drug Information
Absorption
Bioavailability	The absolute bioavailability of NALL following oral administration has not been determined.
Tmax	After oral administration, NALL was absorbed with median (min, max) Tmax of 1 (0.5, 2.5) hour.
Distribution
Volume of distribution	The mean (SD) volume of distribution at steady state (Vss) was 253 (125) L.
Plasma protein binding	Plasma protein binding of NALL is very low with free drug about 100%.
Elimination
Clearance	The mean (SD) clearance is 139 (59) L/hour.
Half-life	The estimated mean half-life is around 1 hour.
Metabolic pathway(s)	Levacetylleucine is metabolized into acetate and L-leucine by ubiquitously expressed enzymes, which are used endogenously in catabolic and metabolic pathways. Cytochrome P450 enzymes are not involved in the metabolism of levacetylleucine.
Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	No mass balance study was conducted in humans. Following a single oral administration to rats, the metabolites are the amino acid L-leucine and acetic acid.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	In the population PK analysis, body weight was identified as a significant covariate; apparent clearance (CL/F) and apparent volume of distribution (V/F) of NALL were lower in subjects with lower body weight. The recommended dosing regimen of NALL in patients with NPC is based on body weight.
Age	Age was not identified as significant covariate affecting NALL PK in the population PK analyses.
Renal impairment	The effect of renal impairment on the PK of IB1001 has not been evaluated. See Section 8.1.
Hepatic impairment	The effect of hepatic impairment on the PK of IB1001 has not been evaluated. See Section 8.1.
Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	No in vitro induction towards CYP enzymes was observed. No clinical drug-drug interaction studies have been conducted.
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Inhibition of efflux transporters P-gp, BCRP, BSEP and OAT1/3 by NALL was observed in vitro.

L153: ## 6.1.2. Dose Selection Rationale for Clinical Trials
L154: The dose of levacetylleucine used in the clinical trials – approximately 0.1 g/kg/day – was chosen based on extrapolation from experience with the racemate N-acetyl-DL-leucine and in vivo studies in relevant animal models (including the NPC mouse model). The Applicant claimed that this dose was supported by the exposure safety margins demonstrated in nonclinical studies. The clinical dose selected was well-tolerated in the trials (i.e., a lower dose was not needed due to any toxicity concerns). The Applicant claimed that long-term clinical data from oral use of the racemate up to 8 g/day in subjects with NPC have shown the treatment to be safe and well-tolerated. Therefore, the Applicant chose the dose of up to 4 g/day.
L155: Based on the similar dose selection rationale, the Applicant chose the 4 g/day dose of levacetylleucine for subjects aged 13 years and older, and approximately 0.1 g/kg/day of levacetylleucine for subjects aged 4 to under 13 years. Therefore, the selected dose for both the
L156: phase 2 and phase 3 clinical trials was 4 g/day for subjects aged 13 years and over. Subjects under the age of 13 years were dosed based on body weight, as follows: children weighing 15 kg to under 25 kg received 2 g/day, children weighing 25 kg to under 35 kg received 3 g/day, and children weighing 35 kg and above received 4 g/day.
L157: The efficacy of the clinical dose was consistent with the nonclinical pharmacology exposure range data and was clinically relevant when compared to placebo treatment. Exposure-response (E-R) analyses for efficacy did not reveal any apparent correlation between plasma exposures and drug effects.
L158: Subjects weighing &lt;15 kg were not enrolled in any of the Applicant's trials.
L159: ## 6.1.3. Exposure-Response Relationship for Efficacy
L160: In the E-R analysis conducted based on the data from 38 subjects who had both efficacy and evaluable pharmacokinetic (PK) data in Trial IB1001-301, no E-R relationship was observed between the functional scale for assessment and rating of ataxia (fSARA) and the exposure metric, daily area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) at steady state at the proposed dosing regimen (Figure 1). However, due to the limited sample size, the E-R relationship for efficacy is inconclusive.

L163: ## 6.1.4. Exposure-Response Relationship for Safety
L164: Due to the limited incident rate for each treatment-emergent adverse event (TEAE) (refer to Section 7.6.1.5), E-R analysis for safety was not performed.
L165: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended To Demonstrate Efficacy
L166: The Applicant's clinical trials to investigate levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC consist of two studies: a multicenter, open-label, single arm study, Trial IB1001-201, and a pivotal placebo-controlled, randomized, crossover study, Trial IB1001-301. Trial IB1001-301 is considered a single adequate and well-controlled trial and will be the primary source to establish effectiveness.
L167: ## 6.2.1. Trial IB1001-201
L168: ## 6.2.1.1. Design
L169: ## Parent Study
L170: Figure 2. Parent Study Design, Trial IB1001-201
L171: ## A. Naïve Subjects
L172: ## B. Non-Naïve Subjects
L173: Source: Trial IB1001-201 Clinical Study Report, page 3-4
L174: ## NDA 219132
## Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L175: Trial IB1001-201 was a multinational, multicenter, open-label, single-arm phase 2 study. A total of 34 participants were screened, of whom 33 (97.1%) were treated with levacetylleucine. One subject was considered a screen failure due to violation of inclusion/exclusion criteria. The parent study of Trial IB1001-201 consisted of a 2-week baseline period, a 6-week treatment period, and a 6-week washout period. Two visits were planned for each period. Due to coronavirus disease of 2019 (COVID-19) restrictions, Visit 3 and Visit 5 were classified as non-essential visits and were allowed to be skipped, while Visit 4 and Visit 6 remained essential visits.
L176: At the initial screening visit, subjects were classified as either naïve or non-naïve based on the use of prohibited medication within the previous 42 days. For naïve subjects, the initial screening visit was treated as Baseline 1. For non-naïve subjects, the initial screening visit was treated as Visit 0 and the subjects underwent a minimum of 42 days of washout before Visit 1 (Baseline 1). During the treatment period, all subjects received 1000 mg powder for oral suspension in 60 mL glass bottles for approximately 42 days (+7 days).
L177: The primary efficacy endpoint of the parent study was based on the blinded raters' clinical impression of change in severity (CI-CS) score comparing paired videos of the subject's performance on the predefined anchor test of either the 9-Hole Peg Test of the dominant hand (9HPT-D) or the 8-meter walk test (8MWT) at two visits. At Visit 1, the investigating physician selected a primary anchor test for each subject based on the predefined criteria. See Section 16.1 for the criteria.
L178: For the CI-CS assessment, two independent, primary raters were provided with 3 pairs of video recordings and asked to assess for each video pair: 'Compared to the first video, how has the severity of their performance on the 9HPT-D or 8MWT changed (improved or worsened) in 6-weeks as observed in the second video?' The video pairs that were made from videos at Visit 2, Visit 4, and Visit 6 were presented in random order, and the internal pairing of each video set was randomized. The raters were blinded to the timepoint of videos. The two primary raters' scores were averaged if there was a 1-point difference between the scores given by the two raters. If a difference was greater than 1 point, a third blinded rater reviewed the assessments and determined the most accurate assessment between the two scores.
L179: The primary endpoint was the CI-CS comparing the end of treatment (Visit 4) with baseline (Visit 2) minus the CI-CS comparing the end of washout (Visit 6) with the end of treatment (Visit 4). Each CI-CS score is measured on a 7-point Likert scale (-3=significantly worse to +3=significantly improved).
L180: While the evaluation of the primary endpoint CI-CS and a secondary endpoint Clinical Impression of Severity were performed by two blinded, independent neurologists based on videos of the anchor test, other secondary endpoints were assessed without blinding.
L181: See Section 16 for the secondary and exploratory endpoints of the parent study.
L182: **Figure 3. Extension Phase**
L183: Source: Trial IB1001-201 Clinical Study Report, page 4
Abbreviations: EOS = end of study; EP = extension phase
L184: Upon completion of the parent study, eligible subjects were offered to participate in the extension phase (EP) that allows subjects to continue treatment with levacetylleucine for approximately 2 years. The EP comprises 6 visits over a 116-week period. The first visit of the EP (Visit 7) was conducted 1 day after Visit 6 at the end of the parent study. All subjects were planned to receive levacetylleucine for 365 days of EP Treatment Period I, followed by 42 days of a washout period. After the washout period, subjects were to receive levacetylleucine for 365 days of EP Treatment Period II.
L185: All endpoints in the EP were considered secondary to those in the parent study. The primary endpoint in this EP was based on the 5-domain Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale (5DNPCCSS) from the EP baseline (Visit 7) to the end of treatment in the EP (Visit 9). The 5-domain NPCCSS is comprised of 5 major domains (ambulation, speech, swallow, fine motor skills, cognition), which add up to a composite value.

L189: Individuals who met all the following criteria were eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-201:
L190: - Written informed consent signed by the patient and/or their legal representative/parent/impartial witness.
- Male or female aged \ge 6 years in Europe **OR** \ge 18 years in the United States with a confirmed diagnosis of NPC at the time of signing informed consent. For individuals in Europe confirmed diagnosis included:Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test without genetic test results (had not been performed).
- Clinical features and positive genetic test.
- Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test but only one NPC mutation identified on genetic test.
- Clinical features with positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test and positive genetic test.
- Females of childbearing potential, defined as a premenopausal female capable of becoming pregnant, were included if they were either sexually inactive (sexually abstinent for 14 days prior to the first dose and confirmed to continue through 28 days after the last dose) or using
L191: one of the following highly effective contraceptives (i.e., resulting in &lt;1% failure rate when used consistently and correctly) 14 days prior to the first dose continuing through 28 days after the last dose:
L192: - Intrauterine device
- Surgical sterilization of the partner (vasectomy for 6 months minimum)
- Combined (estrogen or progestogen containing) hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, intravaginal, or transdermal)
- Progestogen only hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, injectable, or implantable)
- Intrauterine hormone releasing system
- Bilateral tubal occlusion
- Females of non-childbearing potential had to have undergone one of the following sterilization procedures at least 6 months prior to the first dose:Hysteroscopic sterilization
- Bilateral tubal ligation or bilateral salpingectomy
- Hysterectomy
- Bilateral oophorectomy
**OR** be postmenopausal with amenorrhea for at least 1 year prior to the first dose and follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH) serum levels consistent with postmenopausal status. FSH analysis for postmenopausal women was done at screening. FSH levels should have been in the postmenopausal range as determined by the central laboratory.

- Non-vasectomized male patients agreed to use a condom with spermicide or abstain from sexual intercourse during the study until 90 days beyond the last dose of study medication and the female partner agreed to comply with inclusion criteria 3 or 4. For a vasectomized male who had his vasectomy 6 months or more prior to study start, it was required that they use a condom during sexual intercourse. A male who had been vasectomized less than 6 months prior to study start had to follow the same restrictions as a non-vasectomized male.
- If male, patient agreed not to donate sperm from the first dose until 90 days after their last dose.
- Patients had to fall within:A scale for the assessment and rating of ataxia (SARA) score of 5 \le X \le 33 points (out of 40).
**AND**

- Either:Within the 2 to 7 range (0 to 8 range) of the Gait subtest of the SARA scale.
**OR**

- Were able to perform the 9HPT-D (spinocerebellar ataxia functional index [SCAFI] subtest) in 20 \le X \le 150 seconds.
- Weight \ge 15 kg at screening.
L193: - Patients were willing to disclose their existing medications/therapies for (the symptoms) of NPC, including those on the prohibited medication list. Non-prohibited medications/therapies (e.g., miglustat, concomitant speech therapy, and physiotherapy) were permitted provided:The Investigator did not believe the medication/therapy would interfere with the trial protocol/results.
- Patients had been on a stable dose/duration and type of therapy for at least 42 days before Visit 1 (Baseline 1).
- Patients were willing to maintain a stable dose/would not change their therapy throughout the duration of the study.
- Had an understanding of the implications of study participation, provided in the written patient information and informed consent by patients or their legal representative/parent, and demonstrated a willingness to comply with instructions and attend required study visits (for children this criterion would also be assessed in parents or appointed guardians).
L194: ## **Exclusion Criteria**
L195: Individuals who met any of the following criteria were not eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-201:
L196: - Asymptomatic patients.
- Patient had clinical features of NPC and a positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test, but a completely negative result on a previous genetic test for NPC.
- Patients who had any of the following:Chronic diarrhea
- Unexplained visual loss
- Malignancies
- Insulin-dependent diabetes mellitus
- Known history of hypersensitivity to the Acetyl-Leucine (DL-, L-, D-) or derivatives
- History of known hypersensitivity to excipients of Ora-Blend® (namely (b) (4) sorbitol, cellulose, carboxymethylcellulose, xanthan gum, carrageenan, dimethicone, methylparaben, and potassium sorbate)
- Simultaneous participation in another clinical trial or participation in any clinical trial involving administration of an investigational medicinal product ([IMP]; 'study drug') within 6 weeks prior to Visit 1.
- Patients with a physical or psychiatric condition which, at the Investigator's discretion, could put the patient at risk, confound the study results, or interfere with the patient's participation in the clinical trial.
- Known clinically significant (CS) (at the discretion of the Investigator) laboratory findings in hematology, coagulation, clinical chemistry, or urinalysis, including, but not limited to:Aspartate aminotransferase (AST) or alanine aminotransferase (ALT) &gt;5x upper limit of normal (ULN).
- Total bilirubin &gt;1.5x ULN, unless Gilbert's syndrome is present in which case total bilirubin &gt;2x ULN.
- Known or persistent use, misuse, or dependency of medication, drugs, or alcohol.
- Current or planned pregnancy or women who were breastfeeding.

L203: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan
L204: The statistical analysis plan (SAP) was finalized on January 26, 2023 (Version 4.0) before database lock on April 5, 2023. The data cut-off date for efficacy analyses of the parent study was June 22, 2022. For the parent study, analyses were planned in the SAP Version 2.0 dated
L205: May 20, 2020 and its COVID-19 addendum Version 1.0 dated September 16, 2020. For the EP, analyses were planned in SAP Version 4.0.
L206: According to the SAP, the intent-to-treat (ITT) population consists of all subjects who receive at least one dose of levacetylleucine and have a video recording at either Visit 1 or Visit 2, or both. The modified intent-to-treat (mITT) population is defined as all subjects in the ITT population with one video recording at either Visit 3 or Visit 4, or both. The Per Protocol Set consists of all subjects with at least one video recording at baseline (Visit 1 or Visit 2), end of treatment (Visit 3 or Visit 4), and end of washout (Visit 5 or Visit 6), and without any major protocol deviation that could have influenced the validity of the data for the primary efficacy variable.
L207: The primary analysis population for the primary and all secondary efficacy endpoints was the mITT population. A last observation carried forward (LOCF) approach was used within each period if a video is missing or unreadable. Also, if either Visit 3 or Visit 4 was available but both Visit 5 and Visit 6 were missing or unreadable, the CI-CS score was assigned the value 0 (stable) for the primary efficacy analysis.

L209: ## 6.2.1.4.1. Subject Disposition and Baseline Characteristics
L210: ## Parent Study
L211: A total of 33 subjects were enrolled in the parent study of Trial IB1001-201 and treated across 9 sites in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, Spain, and Slovakia. Table 8 presents subject disposition. Two subjects (6.1%) withdrew from the parent study. Of those two, one subject was withdrawn after Visit 2 due to an AE (increased seizure [n=1; 3.0%]) and was not included in the mITT population. This subject began to have mild seizures after 9 days of treatment. Given their medical history of seizures, the AE was not considered related to study drug. The other subject was withdrawn after Visit 3 due to “Other” reason (“Intake of study drug did not lead to benefit” [n=1; 3.0%]).

L224: ## **Extension Phase**
L225: In addition to the 2 subjects who discontinued the parent study, 12 subjects did not consent to the EP. The individual trajectories of endpoints in the discontinued subjects did not show trends of change in either direction before discontinuation. Thus, a total of 19 subjects continued into the EP across 8 sites in the United States, the United Kingdom, Germany, and Slovakia. Of the 19 treated subjects, 17 (89.5%) subjects completed Visit 10 (EP Treatment Period I) and 13 subjects (68.4%) completed Visit 12 (EP Treatment Period II). Of 19 treated subjects in the EP, 11 (57.9%) subjects were male and 8 (42.1%) subjects were female. The overall mean age of the subjects was 24.3 years (SD=11.8).
L226: A total of 6 subjects withdrew from the EP due to withdrawal of consent (n=1; 5.3%), lost to follow-up (n=1; 5.3%), and “Other” (“patient was not receiving any benefit” [n=2; 10.5%], “treatment was considered not sufficient enough” [n=1; 5.3%], or “patient wishes to withdraw from study participation” [n=1; 5.3%]).
L227: **Review team's comment:** The first visit of the first subject of Trial IB1001-201 EP was [REDACTED](b) (6) and the last visit of the last subject was [REDACTED](b) (6). Given that the review team could not identify any pattern regarding study discontinuation, it is considered that COVID-19 restrictions could likely have impacted the loss of sample size in the EP. Consequently, the review will focus on the efficacy results from the parent study, which preserves the majority of the enrolled subjects.
L228: ## **6.2.1.4.2. Efficacy Results for CI-CS**
L229: The results of the Applicant's analysis of the primary efficacy endpoint are summarized in Table 10. The analyses of CI-CS were based on the prespecified mITT population that includes all subjects in the ITT population with one recorded video at either Visit 3 or Visit 4, or both. The mITT set includes 28 subjects who have completed all essential visits of the parent study, 3 subjects who have completed Visit 4 (end of treatment period) but have Visit 6 excluded from the analysis due to extremely delayed visits, and 1 early termination subject who was withdrawn after Visit 3 due to an unrelated serious adverse event (SAE). Within the mITT population, the 9HPT-D was selected for 20 subjects (62.5%) and the 8MWT was selected for 12 subjects (37.5%). The mean CI-CS score in the treatment period (Visit 4 versus Visit 2) was 0.5 (SD=1.3) indicating improvement in the subjects' condition at the end of the treatment period (EOT). The mean CI-CS score in the washout period (Visit 6 versus Visit 4) was -0.4 (SD=1.4) indicating deterioration in the subjects' condition at the end of the washout period (EOW).
L230: The Applicant provided results based on the Wilcoxon signed-rank test (one-sided p-value =0.03) and the Hodges-Lehmann estimate (median of the difference =1, 90% confidence interval (CI): 0.25, 1.75). The estimated difference in mean CI-CS between the two treatment periods was 0.9 with the p-value of 0.03 based on the one-sample t-test.

L247: ## 6.2.1.4.3. Efficacy Results for 8-Meter Walk Test and 9-Hole Peg Test of the Dominant Hand
L248: In addition to evaluation of the subject's performance regarding quality of walking and upper extremity function using the rater-blinded CI-CS endpoint, the quantitative analyses based on speed of gait and dexterity were assessed using the timed 8MWT and 9HPT-D. Both tests were provided as subtests of a secondary endpoint, SCAFI, which was assessed at every visit in the parent study except Visit 0 (screening visit). For each subtest, subjects performed two consecutive trials (Trial 1 and Trial 2). Time was reported within 0.1 second. At every visit, Trial 2 of the 8MWT and 9HPT-D were videoed as the CI-CS primary anchor test.
L249: According to the CI-CS administration and scoring manual version 3.0 dated January 21, 2020, the 8MWT was still administered and video-recorded even if a subject required the use of assistive devices, another person, or wall for support. Per the SAP, if a subject required the unallowable support (another person or wall), the time for the trial was not recorded in the SCAFI scoring but only in the CI-CS scoring.
L250: **Table 12** shows descriptive statistics of the timed 8MWT at the EOT and at the EOW. Per the scoring manual, the subjects with assistive devices (n=5) or a remote visit due to COVID-19 restrictions (n=1) were counted as missing data in **Table 12**. For the ITT population, the mean change from baseline in the 8MWT at the EOT was -1.1 seconds (SD=4.1, median=-0.3). The mean change in the 8MWT at the EOW compared with the start of the washout period was -0.3 second (SD=2.8, median=0.3), indicating nearly no change during the washout period.
L251: The subgroup of subjects whose primary anchor test was the 8MWT for the CI-CS endpoint showed a greater improvement in the 8MWT during the treatment period compared with the subgroup whose primary anchor test was 9HPT-D for the CI-CS endpoint. The former subgroup showed a mean change of -3.5 seconds (SD=5.5), and the latter showed a mean change of 0.1 (SD=2.7) during the treatment period. Considering the mean baseline difference between the subgroups (17.7 versus 8.8 seconds) and the extent of changes in the 8MWT within the 9HPT-D subgroup, the 8MWT subgroup is considered to have shown more notable improvement, while the other subgroup already had stable gait function.

L260: ## 6.2.2. Trial IB1001-301
L261: ## 6.2.2.1. Design
L262: The clinical benefit of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC was primarily based on a multinational, randomized, double-blinded, placebo-controlled, crossover phase 3 study, Trial IB1001-301. This trial involves 11 trial sites across 7 non-US countries. The trial consists of three periods: a baseline period, a 12-week first treatment period (“Treatment Period I”), and a 12-week second treatment period (“Treatment Period II”). At the screening visit, subjects were classified as either “naïve” or “non-naïve” based on their use of prohibited medications within the past 42 days. A total of 60 subjects aged \ge 4 years with a confirmed diagnosis of NPC at the time of signing the informed consent were enrolled and randomized in a 1:1 ratio to one of the following treatment sequences:
L263: - Sequence 1 (N=30): levacetylleucine in Treatment Period I, followed by immediate crossover to placebo in Treatment Period II.
- Sequence 2 (N=30): placebo in Treatment Period I, followed by immediate crossover to levacetylleucine in Treatment Period II.
L264: **Figure 4. Study Design, Naïve Subjects, Trial IB1001-301**
L265: Source: Trial IB1001-301 Clinical Study Report, page 31
All of 60 subjects were naïve subjects; they had not used prohibited medications within the past 42 days.
L266: There was no washout period between the two periods. According to Section 2.5.3.1.2 of the submitted Clinical Overview,2 the mean half-life of levacetylleucine is approximately 1 hour with a peak concentration range values between 0.5 to 2.5 hours after single and repeated oral dose. The Applicant stated that no or minor accumulation was observed after repeated administration.
L267: Upon the completion of the parent study, trial participants were offered the opportunity to participate in a 2-year EP if the safety and tolerability during the parent study was considered acceptable by the Investigator. The EP is ongoing, thus data from the EP were not submitted at the time of the NDA submission (January 24, 2024).
L268: 2 Clinical Overview, received January 24, 2024.
L269: The Applicant's primary endpoint for the U.S. marketing application was a modified scale for the assessment and rating of ataxia (mSARA) total score at the end of treatment period (Visit 4 or Visit 6). The SARA is used to assess eight domains: gait, stance, sitting, speech disturbance, finger chase, nose-finger test, fast alternating hand movements, and heel-shin slide. The details on the scores for each domain are presented in Section 16.4. The SARA total score ranges from 0 to 40, where 0 is the best neurological status and 40 is the worst. The mSARA focuses on six domains: gait, speech disturbance, finger-chase test, nose-finger test, fast alternating movements, and heel-shin test. The mSARA score ranges from 0 to 30. For the U.S. marketing application, the original SARA total score was defined as a key secondary endpoint.
L270: **Review team's comment:** The Agency previously communicated to the Applicant that mSARA was not considered clearly interpretable to convey clinical benefit in daily functioning of patients with NPC. The Agency recommended an fSARA which focuses on four domains: gait, stance, sitting, and speech disturbance. For these four domains, the Agency recommended a modification to the scoring so that each domain has five possible scores: 0 (normal), 1, 2, 3, and 4 (worst severity); thus, the total fSARA score ranges from 0 to 16. Detailed regulatory history and the Agency's recommended scores are available in Section 6.3.1. In this review, results based on the fSARA will be considered as primary evidence of efficacy in patients with NPC.
L271: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria
L272: ## **Inclusion Criteria**
L273: Individuals who met all the following criteria were eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-301:
L274: - Written informed consent signed by the patient and/or their legal representative/parent/impartial witness.
- Male or female aged 4 years or older with a confirmed diagnosis of NPC at the time of signing informed consent. Confirmed diagnosis includes one of the following:3Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test without genetic test results (has not been performed).
- Clinical features and positive genetic test.
- Clinical features and positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test but only 1 NPC mutation identified on genetic test.
- Clinical features with positive biomarker screen and/or filipin test and positive genetic test.
L275: 3 Note: The U.S. protocol required a confirmed genetic diagnosis of NPC. In the end, all 60 patients enrolled in the trial ultimately had a confirmed genetic diagnosis of NPC.
L276: 3. Females of childbearing potential, defined as a premenopausal female capable of becoming pregnant, were included if they were either sexually inactive (sexually abstinent4 for 14 days prior to the first dose and confirmed to continue through 28 days after the last dose) or using one of the following highly effective contraceptives (i.e., resulting in &lt;1% failure rate when used consistently and correctly) 14 days prior to the first dose continuing through 28 days after the last dose:

- Intrauterine device
- Surgical sterilization of the partner (vasectomy for 6 months minimum)
- Combined (estrogen or progestogen containing) hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, intravaginal, or transdermal)
- Progestogen only hormonal contraception associated with the inhibition of ovulation (either oral, injectable, or implantable)
- Intrauterine hormone-releasing system
- Bilateral tubal occlusion
4. Females of non-childbearing potential who had to have undergone one of the following sterilization procedures at least 6 months prior to the first dose:

- Hysteroscopic sterilization
- Bilateral salpingectomy
- Hysterectomy
- Bilateral oophorectomy
**OR** be postmenopausal with amenorrhea for at least 1 year prior to the first dose and FSH serum levels consistent with postmenopausal status. FSH analysis for postmenopausal women was done at screening. FSH levels should have been in the postmenopausal range as determined by the central laboratory.

5. Non-vasectomized male patients who agreed to use a condom with spermicide during the trial until 90 days beyond the last dose of study medication and the female partner agreed to comply with inclusion criteria 3 or 4. For a vasectomized male who had his vasectomy 6 months or more prior to study start, it was required that they use a condom during sexual intercourse. A male who had been vasectomized less than 6 months prior to study start had to follow the same restrictions as a non-vasectomized male.

6. If male, patient agreed not to donate sperm from the first dose until 90 days after their last dose.
L277: 4 Sexual abstinence is considered a highly effective method only if defined as refraining from heterosexual intercourse during the entire period of risk associated with the study treatments. In this trial, abstinence was only acceptable if in line with the subject's preferred and usual lifestyle.
L278: Periodic abstinence (calendar, symptothermal, and postovulation methods), withdrawal (coitus interruptus), spermicides only, and lactational amenorrhoea method were not acceptable methods of contraception. As well, female condom and male condom were not to be used together.
L279: 7. Patients had to have fallen within:

- A SARA score of 7 \le x \le 34 points (out of 40).
**AND**
- Either:Within the 2 to 7 range (0 to 8 range) of the gait subtest of the SARA scale.
**OR**
- Were able to perform the 9HPT-D (SCAFI subtest) in 20 \le x \le 150 seconds.
8. Weight \ge 15 kg at screening.

9. Patients were willing to disclose their existing medications/therapies for (the symptoms) of NPC, including those on the prohibited medication list. Non-prohibited medications/therapies (authorized medicines for NPC [e.g., miglustat], speech therapy, and physiotherapy) were permitted, provided:

- The Investigator did not believe the medication/therapy would interfere with the trial protocol/results.
- Patients had been on a stable dose/duration and type of therapy for at least 42 days before Visit 1 (Baseline 1).
- Patients were willing to maintain a stable dose/would not change their therapy throughout the duration of the trial.
10. Had an understanding of the implications of trial participation, provided in the written patient information and informed consent by patients or their legal representative/parent, and demonstrated a willingness to comply with instructions and attend required trial visits (for children this criterion would also be assessed in parents or appointed guardians).
L280: ## Exclusion Criteria
L281: Individuals who met any of the following criteria were not eligible to participate in Trial IB1001-301:
L282: - Patients who had any known hypersensitivity or history of hypersensitivity to:Acetyl-Leucine (DL-, L-, D-) or derivatives.
- Excipients of the levacetylleucine sachet (namely isomalt, hypromellose, and strawberry flavor).
- Excipients of the placebo sachet (namely isomalt, hypromellose, strawberry flavor, (b) (4) (b) (4)).
- Simultaneous participation in another clinical trial or participation in any clinical trial involving administration of an IMP ("study drug") for at least 42 days prior to Visit 1. At the discretion of the Investigator, Medical Monitor, and Sponsor, the washout period for specific IMPs may be longer based on the pharmacological activity and PK of the drug.
- Patients with a physical or psychiatric condition which, at the Investigator's discretion and in consultation with the Medical Monitor and Sponsor (as applicable), could put the patient at risk, confound the study results, or interfere with the patient's participation in the clinical trial, (i.e., reliably perform trial assessments).
L283: - Known or persistent use, misuse, or dependency of medication, drugs, or alcohol.
- Current or planned pregnancy or women who were breastfeeding.
- Patients with severe vision or hearing impairment (that is not corrected by glasses or hearing aids) that, at the Investigator's discretion, interfered with their ability to perform trial assessments.
- Patients who had been diagnosed with arthritis or other musculoskeletal disorders affecting joints, muscles, ligaments, and/or nerves that by themselves affect patient's mobility and, at the Investigator's discretion, interfered with their ability to perform trial assessments.
- Patients unwilling and/or who were not able to undergo a 42-day washout period from any of the following prohibited medication prior to Visit 1 (Baseline 1) and remain without prohibited medication through Visit 6:N-acetyl-DL-leucine (e.g., Tanganil)
- N-acetyl-L-leucine (prohibited if not provided as IMP in Trial IB1001-301)
- Sulfasalazine
- Rosuvastatin
L284: ## 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan
L285: The SAP was finalized on September 29, 2022 (Version 1.0), prior to database lock and unblinding for the parent phase on June 14, 2023. The primary efficacy analysis population was based on the ITT population that consists of all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of IMP (levacetylleucine or placebo). The primary efficacy endpoint, mSARA at Visit 4 or Visit 6, was analyzed using a mixed model for repeated measures (MMRM) with an unstructured covariance structure. The MMRM included treatment, period, sequence, baseline mSARA value at Visit 2, and baseline-by-period interaction as fixed effects.
L286: ## Handling of Treatment Discontinuation and Use of Rescue Medications
L287: Per the SAP, a treatment policy strategy was considered for the primary analysis for the following intercurrent events 1) treatment discontinuation due to AEs, 2) taking the rescue/prohibited medications sulfasalazine or rosuvastatin, or 3) taking of prohibited medications N-acetyl-DL-leucine (Tanganil, racemate of D and L enantiomers) or levacetylleucine as a chemical grade substance or supplied outside of Trial IB1001-301. Therefore, all data collected was used regardless of whether the intercurrent events occurred or not. No subjects had the second and third types of intercurrent events. For missing data, a LOCF on a per-period basis was applied.
L288: Of 60 randomized subjects, 2 subjects had missing Visit 4 or Visit 6. One subject (b) (6) died after trial withdrawal. This subject withdrew from the trial after Visit 3 due to AE (pneumonia aspiration) during Treatment Period I on levacetylleucine. The AE occurred after the subject stopped treatment with levacetylleucine in preparation for the pre-planned procedure for a percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy (PEG) feeding tube placement at a local non-trial site hospital. The subject had not informed the trial site or Investigator about this pre-planned procedure during screening and only informed the Investigator at Visit 3 (b) (6). The complications during the procedure led the subject to be hospitalized and the subject
L289: developed pneumonia. The subject died 88 days after the treatment discontinuation. As this subject discontinued the trial after Visit 3, assessments at essential visits (Visit 4 and Visit 6) were not available.
L290: One subject (b) (6) missed the Visit 6 assessment. On (b) (6) during Visit 6, which was 1 day after the last dose of study drug in the second treatment period (placebo), the Investigator determined that the subject required hospitalization due to worsening of depression. The Investigator confirmed that the subject remained hospitalized for depression (b) (6). The subject had a history of depression and a prior psychiatric hospitalization (b) (6).
L291: The remaining subjects in the trial (n=58) completed both Visit 4 and Visit 6.
L292: **Review team's comment:** The Applicant proposed LOCF per-period basis for missing values in the primary analysis. Therefore, in the primary analysis, the Visit 3 score was carried to Visit 4 for the Subject (b) (6) and the Visit 5 score was carried to Visit 6 for Subject (b) (6). However, a Visit 6 score does not exist for Subject (b) (6) since the subject withdrew from the trial during the first treatment period (levacetylleucine) and died prior to Visit 6. While the SAP specifies a treatment policy strategy to handle withdrawal from study medication due to AEs and states, "Patients who did not provide sufficient data for a LOCF approach were omitted from analysis," Subject (b) (6) was not omitted from the primary analysis and the nonexisting Visit 6 score in the second treatment period was handled by a hypothetical strategy. In other words, the Applicant's MMRM model implicitly imputed the Visit 6 score of Subject (b) (6) under missing-at-random assumption. While the withdrawal of Subject (b) (6) is not considered related to the investigational drug, the review team conducted sensitivity analyses to evaluate robustness of the primary efficacy analysis result based on fSARA (Section 6.2.2.4.3).
L293: ## 6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses
L294: ## 6.2.2.4.1. Subject Disposition and Baseline Characteristics
L295: A total of 64 patients were screened, of whom 60 were enrolled and treated. Four patients had screen failures due to violation of one or more inclusion criteria. Of the 60 treated subjects, 59 (98.3%) subjects completed both treatment periods, and 58 (96.7%) subjects had assessments at the end of both treatment periods (Visit 4 and Visit 6).

L303: Abbreviations: ITT = intent-to-treat; N = number of subjects randomized and treated; n = number of subjects in specified population or group
L304: Demographic baseline characteristics are presented in **Table 16**. The mean age of subjects was 26.4 (SD=15.9) years, ranging from 5 to 67 years old. Overall, 38.3% of subjects were pediatric patients. Most subjects were White (90%), reflecting the demographics of the trial site locations (most trial sites were in Europe). NPC is a genetic disorder, and the pathogenesis and clinical course are not expected to differ amongst different racial and/or ethnic groups. Because there are international management guidelines for NPC (Geberhiwot et al. 2018), there is reasonable similarity in care practices of the European and American subjects. While there were no FDA-approved treatments for NPC, miglustat is approved for NPC in the non-US countries that participated in the trial and is considered standard of care for patients with NPC. In the United States, despite not being approved for NPC, off-label use is common. Most subjects (85%) in the trial used miglustat at baseline. Miglustat is one of the non-prohibited medications; subjects were allowed to take miglustat during the trial as long as they maintained a stable dose.

L311: Subjects randomized to the placebo-levacetylleucine treatment sequence had higher mean baseline (Visit 2) mSARA, fSARA, SARA, and 5DNPCCSS total scores than those who were randomized to the other sequence (Table 17). The 5DNPCCSS total score was a prespecified primary endpoint for the EP of Trial IB1001-301 and has been used as outcome measures in natural history literature for patients with NPC (Yanjanin et al. 2010) (Mengel et al. 2020) (Mengel et al. 2021).
L312: The efficacy endpoints were highly correlated at baseline. The estimated pairwise Spearman correlations between fSARA, mSARA, and SARA total score at baseline were ≥0.91. The estimated Spearman correlations between 5DNPCCSS total score and the total scores of SARA-based endpoints were 0.72 (fSARA), 0.74 (mSARA), and 0.76 (SARA) at baseline. Additional correlation analyses between the endpoints using all available visits are shown in Section 16.2.

L343: There was no washout period between the two treatment periods. To address concerns about the carryover effect, the statistical review team assessed a sequence effect in the primary analysis model. The test for the sequence effect implied that there is no significant carryover effect (p-value: 0.6027). In addition, between-subject comparison was conducted based on the first period data only. The estimated treatment difference in the first period data was -0.35 (95% CI: -0.90, 0.19) with a p-value of 0.202 using a two-sample t-test adjusting for baseline. While the between-subject test is not adequately powered, the estimated treatment difference still indicates improvement in fSARA total score with levacetylleucine compared to placebo.
L344: The review team assessed the robustness of the primary efficacy results based on different handling of death and missing data. Subject (b) (6) was randomized to levacetylleucine-placebo sequence, discontinued the trial after Visit 3, and died prior to Visit 6. This subject had the fSARA scores of 11 for both baseline (Visit 2) and Visit 3, showing no changes from baseline before trial discontinuation across all domains in both rescored and original scale. Since Subject (b) (6) did not inform the investigator about the pre-planned surgery at screening, the review team considered that this subject could be removed from the analysis per the eligibility criteria. Using 59 subjects (in which Subject (b) (6) is excluded), there was only a negligible difference in the results compared with the primary analysis results for fSARA. The estimated treatment difference was -0.45 (-0.71, -0.20) with a p-value of &lt;0.001. To evaluate robustness of the primary results to the LOCF per period assumption for Subject (b) (6) the primary analysis was replicated omitting Subject (b) (6) and the best possible fSARA score of 0 for Subject (b) (6) Visit 6 (placebo). The estimated treatment difference was -0.42 (95% CI: -0.70, -0.14) with a p-value of 0.004, confirming the primary analysis. When the primary analysis was performed for completers (n=58), a larger treatment difference was observed (0.48, 95% CI: -0.73, -0.22; p-value &lt;0.001) compared to the primary analysis. This is due to the LOCF assumption for Subject (b) (6) in the primary analysis. To be specific, Subject (b) (6) had observed fSARA scores of 3 at baseline, 3 at Visit 4 (levacetylleucine), and 2 at Visit 5 (placebo). The score of 2 at Visit 5 was carried forward to Visit 6 per the LOCF per period assumption, leading to a treatment difference favoring placebo within this subject in the Applicant's MMRM analysis. Thus, the completer analysis that excludes Subject (b) (6) together with ineligible Subject (b) (6) provides a larger treatment effect than the primary analysis.
L345: The review team also examined the empirical distribution function of the treatment difference at the subject level (Table 20). Among the 58 completers, 22 (38%) subjects had a treatment difference favoring levacetylleucine, 4 (6.9%) subjects had a treatment difference favoring placebo, and 32 (55.2%) subjects had a treatment difference of zero score. The overall estimated treatment difference was -0.5 (95% CI: -0.68, -0.31) with a p-value &lt;0.0006 based on a nonparametric sign test.
