L351: Based on the Clinical Inspection Summary dated July 24, 2024, the review team had a concern regarding data reliability for Visit 6 score of Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) who was taking levacetylleucine at Visit 6. The Visit 6 assessment of this subject was performed remotely over videoconference and the assessments were documented approximately 5 days after Visit 6. In addition, the Investigator made multiple changes to gait, right finger-nose-finger, right heel-shin slide, and left heel-shin slide scores approximately 13 days after the scores were documented on the source record. No justification was provided for the assessment changes. Gait score was changed from 3 to 1 in the original scale, and from 2 to 1 based on the Agency recommended rescoring algorithm. Due to potential recall bias and unjustified score changes, the statistical team conducted a post hoc analysis excluding Subject [REDACTED](b) (6). The analysis excluding both Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6) and [REDACTED](b) (6) provided the similar estimated treatment effect of -0.46 (-0.72, -0.20) with a p-value of &lt;0.001. Despite the concerns with the Visit 6 assessment for Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) the trial appears to be conducted adequately based on the review team's site inspection by Division of Clinical Compliance Evaluation.
L352: ## Subgroup Analysis
L353: Figure 7 shows subgroup analyses based on the fSARA. Overall, the subgroup analyses showed a consistent trend of treatment difference, including for the subgroup of subjects not on background miglustat. For the subgroup of seven subjects with baseline age under 10 years, six subjects did not show treatment difference, while one subject experienced 1-unit improvement when treated with levacetylleucine. Similarly, among the nine subjects who did not take miglustat at baseline, eight subjects did not show treatment difference while one subject experienced 1-unit improvement with levacetylleucine.
L354: In addition to demographic subgroup analyses, the statistical review team conducted domain-specific analyses for gait, stance, sitting, and speech disturbance domains. The observed treatment differences between levacetylleucine and placebo for rescored domains fSARA were -0.07 (95% CI: -0.17, 0.03) for gait, -0.13 (95% CI: -0.24, -0.03) for stance, -0.13 (95% CI: -0.25, -0.01) for sitting, and -0.13 (95% CI: -0.28, 0.01) for speech disturbance. While the observed difference was minimal in gait, the review team could not find any item that mainly drives the
L355: result for total score. In addition, a consistent trend of treatment difference favoring levacetylleucine was observed in the total score for the four domains scored using the original SARA scale.

L358: ## 6.2.2.4.4. Efficacy Results for Other Efficacy Endpoints
L359: ## Original SARA
L360: For the U.S. marketing application, the original SARA total score was evaluated as a key secondary endpoint. Using the same method as the primary analysis, the results showed an estimated treatment effect of -1.3 (95% CI: -1.9, -0.7) in original SARA total score with levacetylleucine compared to placebo with a p-value of &lt;0.001 (Table 21).
L361: The two-sample t-test adjusting for baseline values based on Period I data showed the estimated treatment difference of -1.39 (95% CI: -2.62, -0.16) with a p-value of 0.03, indicating a greater mean reduction in SARA total score in subjects who received levacetylleucine in the first period compared with those who received placebo.

L378: Abbreviations: 5DNPCCSS = 5-domain Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale; 15DNPCCSS = 15-domain Niemann-Pick disease type C clinical severity scale; CI = confidence interval; fSARA = functional Scale for the Assessment and Rating of Ataxia; mDRS = modified disability rating scale; mSARA = the Applicant's modified Scale for the Assessment and Rating of Ataxia; SARA = Scale for the Assessment and Rating of Ataxia; SCAFI = Spinocerebellar Ataxia Functional Index; SD = standard deviation; SE = standard error
L379: ## 6.3. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L380: ## 6.3.1. Interpretability of the Primary Efficacy Endpoint
L381: ## Issue
L382: There are two issues that lead to uncertainty regarding whether the observed changes in the primary efficacy endpoint reflect a clinically meaningful benefit to patients with NPC. These issues include scoring and interpretability of the SARA and the assessment of clinically meaningful improvement in neurological symptoms, which are discussed below.
L383: ## 6.3.1.1. SARA Domain Selection and Rescoring
L384: The Applicant and FDA differed on which SARA domains reflected pragmatic, noticeable changes that communicated clinical benefit suitable for inclusion in a primary efficacy endpoint
L385: (b) (4)
L386: ## Background
L387: The Agency recommended revising the response categories of the SARA so that the domain response options are distinct and clearly interpretable. The Agency recommended using only the four SARA domains of gait, stance, sitting, and speech (i.e., SARA domains #1, #2, #3, and #4, respectively; advice letter dated December 20, 2021; advice letter dated March 21, 2022). The Applicant defined the primary efficacy endpoint in Trial IB1001-301 based on the mSARA, which comprises 6 or the 8 domains evaluated by the SARA: gait (domain 1), speech disturbance (domain 4), finger chase (domain 5), nose-finger test (domain 6), fast alternating hand movement (domain 7), and heel-shin slide (domain 8). The Applicant stated that NPC clinical experts and patients with NPC/their caregivers indicated that fine motor, ambulation, and speech were the top NPC outcomes to measure, that the selected 6 domains accounted for 90% of the improvements observed in the phase 2 study, and that NPC experts indicated changes in SARA domains 5 through 8 were considered to be associated with functional activities for patients with NPC. Postural balance (sitting, stance) was not accounted for among responses, reflecting expert consensus that it is not a key outcome. Scoring of the SARA domains varies (e.g., gait comprises 9 levels, speech comprises 7 levels), which interferes with the interpretation of the total score.
L388: ## Assessment
L389: Consistent with Agency's approach in other rare progressive neurodegenerative indications (Potashman et al. 2024b), several domains of the SARA (finger chase, nose to finger, fast alternating hand movements, heel to shin slide) assess neurologic exam findings. Evidence demonstrating that a change in SARA domains 5 through 8 was directly associated with functional activities for patients was not available for review. These neurologic exam findings do not directly assess the patient's ability to function, nor do they reflect clinically meaningful change in a patient's ability to perform daily functions, making them unsuitable for inclusion in a primary efficacy endpoint to convey clinical benefit. The Agency acknowledges that postural control is not reported as an important outcome in NPC (Parseghian 2019); however, the measurement of sitting and stance are functional abilities where a change in scores clearly
L390: correspond to daily functioning. As the Applicant did not revise the response categories of the SARA domains, the Agency rescored to create the same number of response categories across each domain (Table 23) to ensure equal representation of each domain in the total score and interpretability of a 1-point change as clinically meaningful (Potashman et al. 2024a).
L391: ## Conclusion
L392: The Agency accepts the fSARA domains of gait (domain 1), stance (domain 2), sitting (domain 3), and speech (domain 4) with rescoring so that all domains have equal response categories.
L393: ## **6.3.1.2. Interpretability of the SARA in Young Children**
L394: Ataxia and related domains are difficult to measure in young children with a progressive condition, a measurement challenge that is not specific to the SARA. SARA scores can have low reliability in developing young children with progressive conditions, which may interfere with interpretability (Brandsma et al. 2014) (Lawerman et al. 2017). The Applicant indicated inter-child variation in SARA ratings may be mitigated by the cross-over study design; however, the small population of subjects &lt;10 years old made interpretation challenging.
L395: ## Conclusion
L396: Establishing efficacy in pediatric subjects &lt;10 years old will rely on extrapolation from subjects ≥10 years old.

L401: ## 6.3.1.3. Clinically Meaningful Benefit in Neurological Symptoms
L402: There is uncertainty regarding whether the observed treatment effect substantiated a clinically meaningful treatment benefit in neurological symptoms in patients with NPC receiving levacetylleucine.
L403: ## **Background**
L404: In rare disease research, there is often no established estimate of clinically meaningful within-subject change for a specific disease or condition. Here, the Applicant's primary endpoint was constructed using the mSARA. The Applicant's proposed meaningful within-subject change threshold5 to aid in the interpretation of the proposed primary endpoint results in Trial IB1001-301 was based on [REDACTED] (b) (4) and the Applicant's interpretation of the original SARA scale. Specifically, the Applicant identified [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L405: [REDACTED] as representing clinically meaningful change to patients with NPC. The Applicant did not provide justification for the proposed [REDACTED] (b) (4) change on the mSARA score [REDACTED] (b) (4)
L406: Lastly, [REDACTED] to evaluate whether the observed treatment effect was clinically meaningful, the Applicant [REDACTED] (b) (4) and concluded that there is clinically meaningful treatment benefit with levacetylleucine.
L407: ## **Assessment**
L408: Given the issues with the mSARA discussed in Section 6.3.1.1 and the limited qualitative and quantitative evidence to support the Applicant's proposed clinically meaningful within-subject change threshold, the Agency disagrees with proposed clinically meaningful within-subject change threshold [REDACTED] (b) (4) in the mSARA.
L409: The Agency conducted an independent assessment of clinical meaningfulness of the primary efficacy endpoint results based on the fSARA by performing responder analyses, quantitative analyses at the individual subject level, and evaluating exploratory endpoint evidence from exit surveys.
L410: While the Agency recommends the use of anchor-based methods to interpret endpoints for clinical outcome assessments whose scores are not directly interpretable, the review team
L411: 5 Referred to as “clinically important difference” in the Applicant's submission.
L412: determined the fSARA response options represented distinct and meaningfully different health states, thus making the endpoint scores directly interpretable (April 2023) such that a 1-point change in any fSARA domain is indicative of a meaningful change (either improvement or worsening) to patients with NPC (see Section 6.3.1.1). Furthermore, because each domain in the fSARA represents an important aspect of daily functioning, improvement in any domain regardless of the absence of change/presence of worsening in other domains provides independent evidence of meaningful clinical benefit to patients.
L413: The Agency conducted responder analyses and defined a responder as a subject who had at least 1-point improvement from baseline in any of the four fSARA domains (i.e., gait, stance, sitting, and speech). The responder analysis was performed for subjects who completed both Visit 4 and Visit 6. Consequently, Subjects [REDACTED](b) (6) were excluded from the analysis. For brevity, the review team will interchange the term 'treatment period' and 'period'. There were 33 treatment responders (56.8%) in the trial: 13 subjects (22.4%) responded in the levacetylleucine period and not in the placebo period, 2 subjects (3.4%) responded in the placebo period and not in the levacetylleucine period, and 18 subjects responded in both periods (31%). The estimated difference in response rates between the subjects who responded in only one of the treatment periods was 18.9% (95% CI using 10,000 resampling datasets: 6.9%, 31.0%). Among subjects who responded in at least one period during the trial, there were a total of 31 responders (53.4%) in the levacetylleucine period, and 20 responders (34.5%) in the placebo period (Table 24).
L414: To test nonequality of treatment effects between levacetylleucine and placebo in the dichotomous crossover data, the Mainland-Gart test was conducted. Since the number of subjects who contribute to the contingency table is small, we used a Fisher's exact version of the Mainland-Gart test to evaluate non-equality of treatment effects. The p-value of 0.011 suggests that there is strong evidence that the effects on subjects having at least 1-point improvement in any domain are not equal between placebo and levacetylleucine, favoring levacetylleucine.

L429: Additionally, given the heterogeneity of NPC, it is important to evaluate individual changes in the total fSARA and in each of its four domains. For that purpose, dumbbell plots of change from baseline to EOT (i.e., Visit 4 or Visit 6) in fSARA (Figure 8) and each of the four domain scores (Figure 32 to Figure 35 in Section 16.3) were visualized. The dumbbell plot (Figure 8) shows the change from baseline to end of treatment in fSARA during the levacetylleucine period (green circle) and placebo period (purple diamond) for each subject. During the levacetylleucine period, 72.9% of subjects experienced no change in fSARA from baseline compared to 72.4% of subjects during the placebo period. For those who experienced changes, more subjects experienced improvement while in the levacetylleucine period (20.3%) as compared to when they were in the placebo period (15.5%), whereas more subjects experienced worsening while in the placebo period (12.1%) than while in the levacetylleucine period (6.8%). Subjects in general experienced more improvement in the fSARA when they received levacetylleucine as compared to when they received placebo (i.e., the green open circle is on the left of the purple diamond), with few exceptions (e.g., 4 [6.9%] subjects improved more in the fSARA on placebo than when they were in the levacetylleucine period).

Abbreviation: fSARA = functional Scale for the Assessment and Rating of Ataxia
L432: Overall, similar trends of change described above were observed across the four domains. See Section 16.3 for a detailed discussion.
L433: The Applicant conducted exploratory exit surveys at the conclusion of Trial IB1001-301 (i.e., Visit 6 before unblinding) to provide additional evidence of clinical meaningfulness and subject experiences with levacetylleucine. Subjects and caregivers provided reports of improvements in neurological symptoms (e.g., swallowing, reduction in falls, cataplexy, cognition). The exploratory evidence from the exit surveys may be viewed as supportive for the broad treatment effects related to neurological symptoms. From a clinical perspective, NPC is a progressive, neurodegenerative disease where neurological symptom stabilization and/or improvement of any magnitude for any neurological symptom would not be expected given the known natural history of the disease. Patients, caregivers and clinical experts in NPC have
L434: NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L435: indicated that slowing of disease progression as well as improvement in neurological symptoms would be meaningful (Parseghian 2019).
L436: ## Conclusion
L437: While the observed average treatment effect in fSARA is -0.45, at least a 1-point improvement in any of the four fSARA domains were seen more often when subjects received levacetylleucine than received placebo. As improvements in neurological symptoms would not be expected given the known natural history of NPC, this change is considered to be clinically meaningful.
L438: ## 6.3.2. Establishment of Drug Effectiveness in Patients &lt;10 Years Old
L439: ## 6.3.2.1. Body Weight \ge15 kg
L440: ## Issue/Background
L441: Because of the uncertainty regarding the interpretation of the primary efficacy endpoint in subjects &lt;10 years old (body weight \ge15 kg) and the very limited number of studied subjects in this age group, there was a concern regarding the adequacy of the data from Trial IB1001-301 to establish efficacy patients with NPC who are &lt;10 years old.
L442: ## Assessment
L443: Of the 60 randomized subjects in Trial IB1001-301, 7 subjects (11.7%) were &lt;10 years old. According to a subgroup analysis, the observed treatment difference for this subpopulation was -0.14 (95% CI: -0.49, 0.21; Figure 7). Among the 7 subjects &lt;10 years old, only one subject showed a treatment difference between IB1001 and placebo in fSARA score at the end of treatment periods (Figure 9). Due to the small number of subjects in this subgroup, the review team determined that the efficacy data from Trial IB1001-301 were not sufficient to demonstrate a treatment benefit in subjects &lt;10 years old.
L444: **Figure 9. Individual Trajectory of fSARA Total Score at the End of Treatment Period for Subjects &lt;10 Years Old**
L445: Source: The statistical review team's analysis based on adsara2.xpt located at \\CDSESUB1\evsprod\NDA219132\0011\m5\datasets\ib1001-301\analysis\adam\datasets\adsara2.xpt

Subjects (b) (6) were randomized to Sequence 1 (levacetylleucine-placebo) and subjects (b) (6) were randomized to Sequence 2 (placebo-levacetylleucine).

Abbreviation: fSARA = functional Scale for the Assessment and Rating of Ataxia
L446: While there can be predominant visceral manifestations with very early symptom onset, when symptoms begin in the late infantile period or afterward, they are predominantly neurodegenerative. Because NPC is a genetic disease with the same underlying disease pathophysiology across age groups, levacetylleucine is expected to exert its treatment effect on the neurological symptoms of NPC similarly across age groups. Given the shared underlying disease pathophysiology and primary manifestations of NPC in subjects &lt;10 years old with those ≥10 years of age, the review team determined that it would be appropriate to extrapolate the efficacy data from subjects ≥10 years to the younger population from Trial IB1001-301 by comparing PK data between subjects in these two age groups from Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301.
L447: ## PK Exposures &lt;10 Years Old (Body Weight ≥15 kg) vs. ≥10 Years Old
L448: The exposures (daily AUC and maximum plasma concentration [C_{max}]) of IB1001 were estimated for each subject who received the proposed dosing regimen in Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301 and are summarized by age groups (≥10 years and &lt;10 years) in Table 26. The daily AUC and C_{max} of IB1001 in pediatric subjects &lt;10 years of age were generally similar to those in the older pediatric subject group (≥10 years of age). Thus, the overall PK results would provide support to extrapolate efficacy from older pediatric subjects
L449: (\ge 10 years old) to younger pediatric subjects (&lt;10 years old). Of note, the youngest pediatric subject included in the analysis was 6 years old.

L455: ## **Conclusion**
L456: The establishment of efficacy in pediatric patients &lt;10 years old relies on extrapolation of efficacy seen in subjects \ge 10 years old in Trial IB1001-301. The review team determined that extrapolation of efficacy from subjects \ge 10 years old to patients &lt;10 years old is scientifically justified based on the same underlying disease pathophysiology, similarity in clinical manifestations, and the expected same therapeutic mechanism of action (MOA) across age groups. The PK findings from Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301 support this extrapolation, as they provide evidence that the evaluated dose in subjects &lt;10 years old results in similar exposure to subjects \ge 10 years old. Therefore, the efficacy results from Trial IB1001-301 and PK data from Trials IB1001-201, IB1001-202, and IB1001-301 support the effectiveness of IB1001 in patients &lt;10 years old and support a labeled indication for pediatric patients weighing \ge 15 kg (refer to subsection below for a discussion on why the available data do not support dosing in patients &lt;15 kg).
L457: (b) (4)
L458: ## 6.3.3. Adequacy of Confirmatory Evidence
L459: ## Issue/Background
L460: SEE, the regulatory requirement for approval (under 21 CFR 314.126), generally consists of evidence from at least two adequate and well-controlled trials. In certain circumstances, a single adequate and well-controlled trial demonstrating efficacy may be sufficient to generate SEE if there is adequate CE. Such circumstances are described in the FDA draft guidance for industry *Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness for Human Drug and Biological Products* (December 2019), which includes: the persuasiveness of the single investigation; the robustness of the CE; the seriousness of the disease, particularly when there is an unmet medical need; the size of the patient population; and whether it is ethical and practicable to conduct more than one adequate and well-controlled clinical investigation. These criteria and considerations are all relevant to the levacetylleucine development program for NPC, a rare and serious disease with an unmet need. Because the levacetylleucine NDA includes only one adequate and well-controlled trial, the review team sought to assess whether there is adequate CE to generate SEE. As stated in the FDA draft guidance for industry *Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness With One Adequate and Well-Controlled Clinical Investigation and Confirmatory Evidence* (September 2023), the strength of the single adequate and well-controlled clinical trial informs the quantity and quality of CE needed to substantiate the results of the trial. The highly persuasive results of the single trial, Trial IB1001-301 (described in Section 6.2.2.4.3), influenced the review team's determination of the level of strength needed for the data to serve as CE in this application.
L461: The Applicant submitted data from multiple sources to serve as potential CE of the treatment effect of levacetylleucine observed in Trial IB1001-301. These sources, which included nonclinical studies (mechanistic in vitro and animal disease model) and clinical trials (a phase 2 single arm trial in patients with NPC and a phase 2 single arm trial in patients with GM2 gangliosidosis), are discussed in the *Assessment* section below.
L462: ## Assessment
L463: ## **Nonclinical Evidence: Mechanistic Studies (In Vitro)**
L464: Data to support the MOA for levacetylleucine were derived from published literature. The Applicant described data suggesting that in cultured Chinese hamster ovary cells, treatment with levacetylleucine at a concentration of 1mM for 24 hours, was associated with a modest reduction in lysosomal volume, sphingosine, GSLs, and a very modest reduction in stored cholesterol. In NPC patient-derived fibroblasts, treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest reduction in apparent lysosomal volume when cells were cultured for 7 days with 1mM levacetylleucine. These studies suggest that the drug has the potential to reduce stored intracellular lipid; however, the MOA, including any molecular targets by which it may elicit these effects, is unclear.
L465: ## **Nonclinical Evidence (In Vivo)**
L466: In *Npc1-/-* mice, a nonclinical mouse model of NPC, pre-symptomatic (from the age of 3 weeks) treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest increase in survival and a reduction in markers of neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum (see
L467: Section 13.2.2). These changes were not monitored in the clinical trial to evaluate the association of change in magnitude of GSL and neuroinflammation with the dose of levacetylleucine and efficacy. Taken together, the nonclinical data support a potential mechanism by which levacetylleucine may reduce intracellular cholesterol and may improve survival when administered pre-symptomatically in mice; however, because the same parameters were not assessed in clinical trials, there is no direct correlation between these data and the efficacy endpoints measured in subjects.
L468: ## Additional Clinical Data
L469: The additional clinical data submitted by the Applicant as potential sources of clinical CE include the open-label study of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC (Trial IB1001-201), an open-label study of levacetylleucine in individuals with GM2 gangliosidosis (Trial IB1001-202), and several case series detailing off-label use of Tanganil (N-acetyl-DL-leucine) in NPC and GM2 gangliosidosis.
L470: ## Trial IB1001-201
L471: The results from Trial IB1001-201 are based on the rater-blinded CI-CS in the parent study. Thirty-three subjects with NPC were enrolled in the trial, which consisted of a 2-week baseline period, a 6-week treatment period, and a 6-week washout period. The CI-CS measured each subject's change in performance on their primary anchor test (8MWT or 9HPT-D) between the beginning and end of the treatment and washout periods. Improvements were observed in the treatment period for the selected anchor test with the mean CI-CS score of 0.5 (median=1), but performance declined by the EOW with the mean CI-CS score of -0.4 (median=-0.2). The Hodges-Lehmann estimate for the median difference of CI-CS score between the two periods was 1.0 (90% CI: 0.25, 1.75), indicating improvement in the treatment period and deterioration during the washout period. The associated Wilcoxon signed rank test resulted in a one-sided p-value of 0.03. The rater-blinded CI-CS endpoint relied on an investigator-selected primary anchor test. Each anchor test was performed by all subjects as a component of the SCAFI (a secondary endpoint). The timed SCAFI subtests showed minimal changes in 8MWT in both periods, with mean changes of -1.1 and -0.3 seconds for the treatment and washout periods, respectively, which were considered within measurement error. The 9HPT-D showed minor changes in the treatment period with a mean change of 1.1 seconds, but a deterioration with a mean change of 7.7 seconds after the washout.
L472: While Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label study and therefore was susceptible to bias that decreases the persuasiveness of these results, the methodology of blinding the CI-CS raters to time and treatment assignment increases the interpretability of the resulting scores by reducing the influence of knowledge of treatment assignment.
L473: The CI-CS results supported by the timed SCAFI subtests indicate either improvement or stability in the treatment period, and either deterioration or stability in the washout period. Consequently, the Agency concludes that Trial IB1001-201 provided sufficient supportive evidence that the subjects' performance on the selected measures of gait and fine motor function is comparatively favorable in the treatment period. Refer to Section 6.2.1 for detailed results.
L474: NDA 219132
Aqueursa (levacetylleucine)
L475: ## Trial IB1001-202
L476: Trial IB1001-202 was a phase 2 study of levacetylleucine in 30 subjects with GM2 gangliosidosis, a lysosomal storage disorder with some shared pathophysiological mechanisms that could potentially benefit from treatment with levacetylleucine. The trial design was identical to that of Trial IB1001-201 in subjects with NPC, and the results from Trial IB1001-202 are based on the rated-blinded CI-CS in the parent study. Similar to Trial IB1001-201, the Trial IB1001-202 parent study also met its primary endpoint CI-CS. The mean CI-CS in the treatment period was 0.34 (SD=1.59, median=0.50), while the mean CI-CS in the washout period was -0.36 (SD=1.33, median=-0.50). The Hodges-Lehmann estimate was 0.75 (90% CI: 0.00, 1.50), indicating a nominally significant effect for levacetylleucine in terms of improvement during the treatment period followed by deterioration during washout.
L477: Given the similar pathophysiological mechanisms of NPC and GM2 gangliosidosis and the same expected MOA of levacetylleucine in the two diseases, the results from Trial IB1001-202 may provide some support for the effectiveness of levacetylleucine in lysosomal storage disorders such as NPC. However, since levacetylleucine has not been approved in the United States for the treatment of GM2 gangliosidosis (and thus, the efficacy has not been established), the review team determined that the results of IB1001-202 are not adequate to be used as CE of effectiveness of levacetylleucine for NPC.
L478: ## Real World Data
L479: The Applicant submitted three published case series describing the off-label use of Tangamil (N-acetyl-DL-leucine)
L480: The exact number of individual patients with NPC is not clear as several may overlap between the submitted publications. Although these publications provided early proof-of-concept for further evaluation of the components of N-acetyl-DL-leucine as a potential treatment for NPC early in the development of levacetylleucine, they were not considered as supportive data of the findings of Trial IB1001-301
L481: ## Conclusion
L482: The several different sources of data described above were submitted to serve as CE of the treatment benefit of levacetylleucine observed in Trial IB1001-301. Each of these sources of data has considerable limitations, as detailed above, that preclude their acceptability to serve as CE on their own. However, the review team determined that the integration of the clinical data from Trial IB1001-201 along with the nonclinical data together constitute acceptable CE for this application, which included a highly persuasive clinical trial.
L483: As stated in the FDA draft guidance for industry *Demonstrating Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness With One Adequate and Well-Controlled Clinical Investigation and Confirmatory Evidence* (September 2023), the strength of the single adequate and well-controlled clinical trial informs the quantity and quality of CE needed to substantiate the results of the trial. As detailed in Section 6.2.2.4.3, Trial IB1001-301 was considered highly persuasive due to its randomized, blinded, placebo-controlled trial design and because it demonstrated a clinically meaningful
L484: treatment benefit on the primary endpoint (p-value: &lt;0.001). The review team determined that, in the context of these highly persuasive results from the pivotal trial, the strength of the submitted CE – specifically, the results of Trial IB1001-201 and the supportive mechanistic nonclinical data – was adequate to substantiate the results of Trial IB1001-301. Together, Trial IB1001-301 and the CE establish SEE of levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC, a rare disease with an unmet therapeutic need. The data from both the adequate and well-controlled trial (Trial IB1001-301) and the CE are adequate to support use of levacetylleucine either with or without background concomitant miglustat.
L485: ## 6.3.4. Uncertainty Regarding Durability of Treatment Effect
L486: ## Issue/Background
L487: The single adequate and well-controlled trial, Trial IB1001-301, assessed the efficacy of levacetylleucine for NPC over a treatment period of 12 weeks. Because treatment with levacetylleucine beyond 12 weeks occurred only during the OLE periods of Trial IB1001-301 and IB1001-201, the interpretability of the efficacy data to support long-term use was limited.
L488: ## Assessment
L489: For the pivotal Trial IB1001-301, the Applicant initially proposed a placebo-controlled trial duration of 6 weeks based on preliminary findings from their open-label study, Trial IB1001-201. During a Type C meeting held on May 27, 2001, a minimum placebo-controlled duration of 12 weeks for Trial IB1001-301 was advised to the Applicant by the FDA. Trial IB1001-301 incorporated 12 weeks of placebo-controlled treatment as agreed upon, followed by an OLE study that is ongoing. Subjects who completed the trial were eligible to continue open-label treatment with levacetylleucine for up to 2 additional years. After a midcycle meeting with the Applicant in May 2024 during the review of this NDA, the Applicant provided a high-level summary of data for the first year of the extension study. The primary endpoint for the EP is not the same as for the parent study, thus a comparison of trial outcomes from 12 weeks of placebo-controlled treatment to the first year of open-label treatment would be challenging due to the different timing of assessments and prespecified endpoints. The endpoint for the EP is the change in 5DNPCCSS from baseline to the EOT.
L490: The Applicant reported that 54 subjects enrolled in the extension study after completion of Trial IB1001-301 and that all have completed the first 6 months of open-label treatment (Visit 8). After 12 months from randomization baseline, the mean change on the 5DNPCCSS was -0.115 (\pm 2.60). The Applicant compared the mean change to that of published cohorts, however given the lack of individual subject data for either the EP or the cohorts used for comparison, we did not consider this comparison in our assessment. Generally, NPC is expected to progress after the onset of neurological symptoms, thus a mean change that suggests decrease (rather than a mean increase, which would suggest worsening) of 5DNPCCSS scores may indicate potential disease stabilization and/or improvement in a portion of trial subjects, but this interpretation is limited by the absence of a control group.
L491: The Applicant also provided summary data from their version of the mSARA, the total SARA, and the fSARA, (b) (4)
L492: (b) (4) This information is limited by the lack of a control group as noted above, as well as by the different time periods between treatment groups in terms of their open-label treatment baseline (i.e., depending on whether they were on treatment during the first or second treatment periods of the parent study), and because the high-level and summary nature of this information could not be independently verified by the FDA.
L493: Trial IB1001-201 included a 6-week initial treatment period with a rater-blinded primary endpoint, followed by continued open-label treatment with levacetylleucine for up to a total of 2 years (with a 6-week washout period between years 1 and 2 of additional open-label treatment). The primary endpoint for the EP of Trial IB1001-201 was also the change in 5DNPCCSS from baseline to the EOT. A total of 19 subjects enrolled in the EP and 13 subjects completed 2 additional years of treatment. After 12 months of treatment with levacetylleucine from the start of the EP, the mean change on the 5DNPCCSS for those with available assessments at baseline (Visit 7) and Visit 10 was 0.88 (SD=2.60) based on 17 subjects. After 2 years of EP (Visit 7 versus Visit 12), the mean change on the 5DNPCCSS was 1.75 (SD=2.56) based on 12 subjects.
L494: As detailed in Section 6.2.1, there was a high drop-out rate for the trial that was likely impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic and associated health concerns as well as travel and activity restrictions, thus the results of the EP are difficult to interpret. Additionally, because the neurological symptoms of NPC are expected to progress, a worsening in 5DNPCCSS scores over the course of 2 years is not unexpected; however, without a concurrent control group for comparison, we could not make definitive conclusions regarding the results of the EP of Trial IB1001-201.
L495: ## Conclusion
L496: The 12 weeks of placebo-controlled data from Trial IB1001-301 followed by high-level summary data from the EP suggesting continued mean improvement and/or relative stability in neurological symptoms after 1 year provide reasonable scientific justification for the long-term use of levacetylleucine. Although some uncertainty remains regarding the long-term efficacy (greater than one year) of levacetylleucine, the data are adequate to support a labeled indication for chronic therapy.
L497: ## 6.3.5. Proposed Indication
L498: ## Issue/Background
L499: (b) (4)
L500: The Applicant proposed an indication
L501: (b) (4) During the Midcycle review meeting on May 8, 2024, the Agency noted
L502: (b) (4)
L503: ## Assessment
L504: (b) (4)
L505: At the request of the Agency, the Applicant provided their justification indication in a Clinical Information Amendment on May 17, 2024.
L506: (b) (4)
L507: As detailed in Section 6.3.1, the 4-domain fSARA that assesses gait, stance, sitting, and speech disturbance with modified scoring is the Agency-recommended approach to using the SARA for the endpoint and efficacy analyses in Trial IB1001-301. (b) (4)
L508: The Agency independently applied the Applicant's approach of examining each SARA domain and considering the neurological features and functional changes measured for domains 1 to 4 of the fSARA. Whether these changes may be influenced by other neurological symptoms of NPC, rather than ataxia alone, was then considered. The symptoms in subjects with late-infantile onset of disease or later (&gt;2 years of age) are primarily neurological, including: ataxia, vertical supranuclear gaze palsy, dysarthria, cognitive impairment, dysphagia, dystonia, seizures, cataplexy, and hypotonia (Patterson 1993). For each of the 4 fSARA domains, there are score response options that could reflect either ataxia or another neurological symptom (or, in many cases, likely a combination of several neurological symptoms). Examples are tandem gait or standing with feet together being impacted by dystonia, sway while sitting being impacted by low truncal tone, and speech being impacted by a combination of cognitive impairment and orofacial dyskinesia. The SARA was originally intended to evaluate cerebellar ataxia and was developed in patients with spinocerebellar ataxia. While each category is geared toward identifying features of ataxia specifically, in patients with NPC, there is more likely a combination of ataxia and other heterogeneous neurological symptoms contributing to their scores.
L509: With respect to the other, non-neurological symptoms of NPC, the most common systemic symptoms include neonatal jaundice, hepatomegaly, and splenomegaly. In general, the visceral symptoms tend to predominate and are more severe with early onset of disease symptoms. However, while the proportion of systemic manifestations declines with late age of onset, some patients have a history of neonatal jaundice and/or hepatosplenomegaly as early as in infancy, before the onset of neurological symptoms as late as in adulthood. The systemic manifestations of NPC were not assessed in Trial IB1001-301. Finally, the inclusion criteria for Trial IB1001-301 selected for subjects with neurological symptoms defined as a SARA score of 7 to 34 (out of 40) points and either a score of 2 to 7 (0 to 8) on the SARA Gait domain or were able to perform the 9-Hole Peg Test in 20 to 150 seconds.
L510: In terms of the proposed MOA of levacetylleucine and nonclinical studies that include published data from studies of *Npc1*-/- mice treated with levacetylleucine that had reduced ataxia and improved gait and mobility, the impact of levacetylleucine on the neurological symptoms of NPC as evidenced by Trial IB1001-301 is plausible.
L511: ## Conclusion
L512: Trial IB1001-301 provides evidence of a treatment benefit for the neurological symptoms of NPC. The SARA scale from which the primary endpoint was constructed was developed to measure the symptoms of cerebellar ataxia specifically. However, in the context of different aspects of pragmatic neurological function measured by the fSARA specifically, the data are adequate to support an indication that addresses the neurological manifestations of NPC. It is not known what impact (if any) levacetylleucine has on the non-neurological symptoms of NPC.
L513: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L514: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L515: - There were no toxicologically significant findings in the 26-week rat and 39-week dog oral gavage toxicology studies conducted with levacetylleucine. Safety margins achieved at the no-observed-adverse-effect levels were 2- to 3-fold in rats and 6- to 9-fold in dogs compared to the steady-state AUCs in humans at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD).
- Embryofetal development toxicity, including malformations and embryofetal death, was observed based on dose-ranging embryofetal developmental toxicology studies that were conducted in rats and rabbits at 1.4- and 6-fold the MRHD based on body surface area.
- Juvenile animal studies have not been conducted; therefore, it is unknown whether levacetylleucine may affect developmental processes in children.
- Nonclinical fertility studies have not been conducted; therefore, risks to male and female fertility and early embryonic development are unknown.
- Nonclinical pre- and postnatal development toxicity studies have not been conducted; therefore, risks to pre- and postnatal development are unknown.
- Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been performed; therefore, carcinogenic risks are unknown.
L516: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L517: Not applicable. There are no other drugs in the same class as levacetylleucine.
L518: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L519: Not applicable. This product has not been approved in the United States or in any foreign markets. Therefore, no postmarketing experience is available at the time of this review.
L520: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L521: Safety data from the trials listed in Table 3 were submitted to support the safety of levacetylleucine. The safety review was primarily based on Trial IB1001-301, the only placebo-controlled study of levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC. Safety results from the open-label, rater-blinded Trial IB1001-201 and the OLEs of both Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201 were reviewed separately to evaluate any potential safety signals not identified in the pivotal trial or that occurred after a longer duration of exposure to levacetylleucine. Lastly, summary data from the completed open-label study of levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis (Trial IB1001-202) was reviewed for additional safety signals, withdrawals, or deaths. An ongoing open-label study of levacetylleucine in Ataxia-Telangiectasia (Trial IB1001-203) was not included because no summary data were provided by the Applicant.
L522: Because there are no clinical safety data for levacetylleucine in subjects under 5 years old, the Agency requested any available real-world data from the literature or from treating clinicians known to the Applicant. The Applicant did not have any available data for levacetylleucine but did provide data supporting the safety of the racemate of levacetylleucine, Tanganil (N-acetyl-DL-leucine) in patients with NPC and GM2 gangliosidosis from a patient registry and a clinician's anecdotal reports. Medical literature including several case reports and case series summarizing off-label use of Tanganil in NPC and other conditions that was included in the NDA submission by the Applicant was not included in the safety evaluation because subject-level data for the age groups represented (which were primarily adults) was available for levacetylleucine in the safety database.
L523: The labelled adverse reactions (ARs) for Tanganil were reviewed as AEs that may overlap with levacetylleucine (skin rashes, urticaria). However, the form of Tanganil marketed in France and other countries uses a wheat-based excipient and is contraindicated in patients with a wheat allergy; thus, it cannot be determined if ARs are related to the excipient rather than the active ingredient. The majority (87%) of subjects in Trial IB1001-301 were taking concomitant miglustat as part of their standard of care therapy and were on a stable dose at the time of enrollment. Labelling for Zavesca (miglustat) for adults with Gaucher disease contains 'Warnings and Precautions' for the following: tremor, gastrointestinal events such as diarrhea and weight loss, peripheral neuropathy, and thrombocytopenia. We considered the potential impact of concomitant miglustat use on reported ARs with levacetylleucine exposure throughout our review.

L533: The NPC safety population (including Trials IB1001-301 and IB1001-201) consisted of a total of 84 subjects who were between 5 to 67 years old. The lowest weight of a subject was 20.6 kg. There were nine subjects who participated in both parent studies and were only counted once for this total. Between the two trials, there were 28 pediatric subjects (7 were under 10 years old) and 56 adult subjects.
L534: First, the data from each trial with submitted datasets was examined for the required and standardized components and for completeness of data. The review team then confirmed that the AEs reported by the Applicant accurately described the AEs reported by the investigators. Once accuracy was confirmed, the frequencies of each AE were assessed. The review team reviewed all deaths, SAEs, discontinuations, and withdrawals.
L535: Trial IB1001-301 was assessed and is reported separately as the only placebo-controlled study of levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC. Trial IB1001-301 is a cross-over study where subjects were randomized 1:1 to either levacetylleucine or placebo in addition to their baseline standard of care for 12 weeks (Treatment Period I), followed by immediate crossover to Treatment Period II, in which subjects previously on placebo began treatment with levacetylleucine and subjects previously on levacetylleucine began treatment with Placebo for 12 weeks. Because there may be carryover effects or repeated AEs (such as worsening of the same AE in the same subject) from Treatment Period I in Treatment Period II, Treatment Period I was the primary portion of Trial IB1001-301 reviewed for common ARs. Eligible subjects who completed Trial IB1001-301 enrolled in an ongoing OLE (IB1001-301) for 2 additional years of treatment, thus Trial IB1001-301 (including Treatment Period I and Treatment Period II) is referred to as the “parent study.” All subjects included in the 120-day safety update for Trial IB1001-301 have completed at least 1 year of open-label treatment (only summary data were available for review of this ongoing trial).
L536: Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label (single arm) study with a rater-blinded primary efficacy endpoint. In the parent study, subjects were treated with levacetylleucine for 6 weeks, followed by a 6-week washout period. Eligible subjects had the option to enroll in an OLE (IB1001-201 OLE) that allowed subjects to continue treatment with levacetylleucine for approximately 2 additional years. IB1001-201 OLE consisted of treatment with levacetylleucine for 1 year (OLE Treatment Period I), followed by a 6-week washout period, and then a second year of treatment with levacetylleucine (OLE Treatment Period II).
L537: In addition, the review team separately assessed summary data from an open-label trial of levacetylleucine in GM2 gangliosidosis, primarily for SAEs, withdrawals, and/or deaths.
L538: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L539: The safety database was adequate for a sufficient safety assessment of levacetylleucine (IB1001) for the indication of NPC in children and adults weighing 15 kg or greater. The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that precluded performing a thorough safety review. No major issues were identified with respect to coding of AEs.
L540: Table 29 shows the baseline demographics of subjects enrolled in Trial IB1001-301, the only placebo-controlled trial in NPC. The two trial arms were generally well-balanced apart from there being fewer pediatric subjects in the placebo arm than in the levacetylleucine treatment arm. There were fewer pediatric participants than adult participants enrolled overall. 23 children
L541: total enrolled, 7 of whom were 10 years old or younger at the time of enrollment. There were 37 adults enrolled. While Trial IB1001-301 intended to enroll patients aged 4 years and older, the youngest participant was 5 years old.
L542: Most subjects (90%) were White, which is not representative of the global population with NPC but may reflect the trial site locations, which were primarily in Europe and Great Britain. Because treatment recommendations and guidelines for NPC are international and the disease manifestations are expected to be similar across different races and ethnic groups, it is not likely that care practices and preferences would differ significantly.

L566: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L567: ## 7.6.1. Safety Findings and Concerns, Trial IB1001-301
L568: ## Overall Summary
L569: The demonstrated safety profile of levacetylleucine is acceptable at the doses intended for marketing. ARs that occurred in two or more subjects were abdominal pain, dysphagia, upper respiratory infections, vomiting, and thrombocytopenia on laboratory analysis.
L570: There were no SAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There was one death of a subject in Trial IB1001-301 that occurred after withdrawal from the trial due to complications from a planned surgical procedure. While the duration of IB1001-301 was relatively short (made up of two 12-week treatment periods in the crossover study design), the data from this study in combination with the other submitted safety data for levacetylleucine are adequate to support
L571: chronic use. The review team concluded that the risks observed are acceptable with appropriate product labeling and routine pharmacovigilance.
L572: ## 7.6.1.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
## Summary, Trial IB1001-301
L573: Table 33 provides a summary of AEs reported during the parent study. To avoid the potential impact of carry over effects from Treatment Period I to Treatment Period II, the risk difference is only presented for Treatment Period I. One or more TEAEs were reported by more subjects exposed to levacetylleucine (IB1001) than placebo during Treatment Period I (63% versus 43%, with a risk difference of 20). In the overall cohort (Treatment Period I and Treatment Period II), there were two SAEs in one subject while randomized to levacetylleucine, one SAE of which was ultimately fatal after withdrawal from the trial.

L582: The most reported TEAE in the overall trial population was upper respiratory tract infection, occurring in 10% of subjects. The most common system organ class with TEAE was infections and infestations (27%). The most common ARs in the overall population were gastrointestinal disorders including abdominal pain, dysphagia, and vomiting (See Section 7.7.1). While there were no reported ARs of thrombocytopenia, laboratory analysis demonstrated thrombocytopenia in a higher portion of subjects on levacetylleucine compared to placebo during Treatment Period I (See Section 7.6.1.6).
L583: ## 7.6.1.2. Deaths, Trial IB1001-301
L584: There was one subject with two SAEs unrelated to the study drug that led to withdrawal from the trial (physician decision) and that ultimately had a fatal outcome during Treatment Period I of the parent study of Trial IB1001-301.
L585: Subject (b) (6) was an adult male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-301. This subject had severe dystonia requiring multiple medications (gabapentin, trihexiphenidyl, clonazepam and a THC/CBD oil) in addition to other symptoms of NPC. The subject was status-post placement of a deep brain stimulator for dystonia and was also taking miglustat. The subject had a planned elective procedure to place a PEG tube on Study Day 67 (the trial staff was not previously aware of this planned procedure). The study drug (levacetylleucine) was stopped for this procedure. After the procedure, the subject experienced an episode of aspiration on Study Day 70 that led to pneumonia, and the subject remained hospitalized while being treated with antibiotics and parenteral nutrition. During this hospitalization the subject also suffered a neurologic regression with dystonic crises and worsening agitation that progressed to psychosis before they were withdrawn from the trial by the trial investigator due to their inability to participate in future trial visits. The subject died from respiratory failure 37 days after being withdrawn from the trial. The SAEs of aspiration pneumonia and psychosis were both assessed as not related to the study medication. This reviewer agrees with this assessment.
L586: ## 7.6.1.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301
L587: The SAEs reported in Trial IB1001-301 are noted in Table 34. There were three SAEs during the parent study in subjects randomized to levacetylleucine: two in Treatment Period I (aspiration pneumonia and psychosis in one subject during the same prolonged hospitalization), and one in Treatment Period II (incisional hernia repair).

L596: ## Aspiration Pneumonia and Psychosis
L597: The SAEs that occurred in Treatment Period I (aspiration pneumonia and psychosis) are described in Section 7.6.1.2. Both occurred in the same subject during a prolonged hospitalization due to complications of a planned medical procedure after the study drug had been stopped. Neither event was assessed as related to study drug. This reviewer agrees with this assessment.
L598: ## Hernia Repair
L599: Subject (b) (6) is an adult male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-301. The subject had a past medical history of a caecum volvulus and ileus operation in addition to his symptoms of NPC. They were randomized to placebo during Treatment Period I and then switched to treatment with levacetylleucine during Treatment Period II. During Treatment Period II (Study Day 136) the subject developed an abdominal wall hernia secondary to his previous abdominal surgeries and required surgical repair, which was followed by an 8-day hospitalization. The subject was discharged home after the procedure on Study Day 144 without incident and continued the study drug during the hospitalization. This event was assessed as not related to the study medication. This reviewer agrees with this assessment.
L600: ## SAEs in Placebo Group During Treatment Period II
L601: Two subjects experienced SAEs while on placebo in Treatment Period II after having received levacetylleucine during Treatment Period I.
L602: ## **Worsening Epilepsy**
L603: Subject (b) (6) is an adolescent male at the time of enrollment. The subject had a past medical history of epilepsy on three anti-seizure medications (levetiracetam, sodium valproate, clobazam). The subject first experienced an increase in duration and frequency of his seizures during Treatment Period I, however they did not require hospitalization, thus this was reported as a severe (i.e., not serious) AE. On Study Day 14 in Treatment Period II, the subject required hospitalization for control of worsening seizures and medication management. During the hospitalization, the subject received benzodiazepines and fluids. The subject also had an AE of aspiration pneumonia during this hospitalization that was attributed to sedation from their medications causing worsening dysphagia. The subject was discharged home on Study Day 30 of Treatment Period II. There were no interruptions in the subject's study drug dosing and this SAE was assessed as not related to study drug. In the setting of their prior history of treatment-refractory epilepsy requiring three anti-seizure medications and with the hospitalization occurring 2 weeks into treatment during Period II while taking placebo, this reviewer concurs that worsening epilepsy is not likely related to the study drug.
L604: ## **Major Depression**
L605: Subject (b) (6) was an adult male at the time of trial enrollment. The subject's past medical history was significant for a prior psychiatric hospitalization in 2013 in addition to other symptoms of NPC. The subject was taking sertraline and amantadine daily and clonazepam and quetiapine as needed for depression. The subject began having worsening symptoms of depression (moderate) during Treatment Period I, when they were randomized to levacetylleucine. On the last day of Treatment Period II (during which the subject received placebo) while being assessed for trial Visit 6, the trial investigator recognized severe symptoms of depression and determined that urgent hospitalization was needed. Symptoms were described as similar to catatonia and the subject experienced a significant decline in daily functioning requiring a prolonged hospitalization. This SAE was assessed as not related to study drug. In the context of this subject's prior history of depression requiring hospitalization and worsening over time (and at the EOT II while on placebo), this reviewer agrees that the SAE major depression is unlikely related to the study drug.
L606: ## **7.6.1.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial IB1001-301**
L607: There were no AEs that led to treatment discontinuation.
L608: ## **7.6.1.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301**
L609: The frequency and severity of TEAEs were evaluated to assess the general safety profile of levacetylleucine. TEAEs are AEs that developed, worsened, or became serious during the treatment period. Overall, 58% of subjects experienced a TEAE while on treatment versus 51% while on placebo. Because there was no washout period between Treatment Period I and Treatment Period II, the assessment of TEAEs is primarily focused on Treatment Period I, when there are no potential carryover effects from treatment in the prior period. L630: During Treatment Period I, TEAEs with a risk difference \ge 6.7 were abdominal pain, dysphagia, nasopharyngitis, upper respiratory tract infections, and vomiting. The review team assessed frequent TEAEs to be ARs if they could be reasonably explained by the proposed MOA of levacetylleucine. While it is not clear how levacetylleucine could lead to an increase in upper respiratory infections directly, upper respiratory infections and nasopharyngitis were included due to the increased risk for dysphagia and known risk of aspiration in this disorder as it may be difficult to assess a cause-and-effect relationship of these symptoms during the course of medical care for NPC while on treatment with levacetylleucine. Seasonal allergies were not considered ARs due to the seasonal nature of the symptoms, which would be difficult to attribute to any of the proposed mechanisms of action for levacetylleucine in subjects with NPC.

L635: ## **Treatment-Emergent Events of Interest**
L636: AEs reported for the racemate of levacetylleucine, Tanganil, in the label in France (skin rash, urticaria, hypersensitivity reactions, anaphylactic shock and laryngeal edema and abdominal pain) as well as those listed in current U.S. labeling for Zavesca (miglustat as approved for adults with Gaucher disease: peripheral neuropathy, tremor, diarrhea, weight loss, and reductions in platelet count) were reviewed with heightened interest and included as ARs if they occurred during treatment with levacetylleucine at a more frequent rate than when treated with placebo. ARs reported during treatment with levacetylleucine that also have been reported in labeling for miglustat are gastrointestinal and metabolic symptoms including vomiting, abdominal pain, and weight loss. One subject who was also taking miglustat experienced an AR of weight loss during Treatment Period I. There were 4 subjects randomized to levacetylleucine with laboratory analysis demonstrating a platelet count of &lt;100 in Treatment Period I compared to 2 subjects on placebo. When comparing decreased platelet counts of the overall trial population during Treatment Periods I and II, there was a similar occurrence of decreased platelet counts in both the treatment and placebo groups, which may reflect background miglustat use and/or underlying disease (see further discussion Laboratory Findings, below). There were more subjects in Treatment Period I who were randomized to placebo (n=3) who had diarrhea (an AR reported for miglustat) than those who were randomized to levacetylleucine (n=1). There were no clear hypersensitivity reactions like those reported with Tanganil in Trial IB1001-301. One subject
L637: experienced an exacerbation of a previous diagnosis of rosacea during Treatment Period II while randomized to levacetylleucine that responded to their typical treatment for rosacea exacerbations and did not require a change in dose or discontinuation of levacetylleucine.
L638: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial IB1001-301
L639: Routine safety monitoring bloodwork (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase) and urinalysis were obtained throughout the trials. The mean values for most of these tests remained within normal ranges except for low platelets (thrombocytopenia) in both the treatment and placebo cohorts as detailed below. There were two instances of decreased potassium during Treatment Period I and one instance of mildly elevated blood urea nitrogen during Treatment Period II that are not likely CS.
L640: ## Hematology
L641: Table 37 demonstrates abnormalities in hematology laboratory values during Trial IB1001-301. There were several isolated low and high white blood cell count values during Period I that are not likely CS, and most other hematology laboratory values were within normal limits except for platelets, discussed in further detail in Thrombocytopenia, below.

L651: ## **Thrombocytopenia**
L652: There were no AEs of thrombocytopenia reported; however, there were subjects in both treatment groups with low platelets, called thrombocytopenia (typically defined as &lt;150 \times 10^3 cells/uL), identified via routine laboratory analysis during the trial. Patients with NPC can have thrombocytopenia related to their underlying diagnosis. Baseline values in Trial IB1001-301 ranged from 65 to 345 \times 10^3 cells/uL (mean 170.45 \times 10^3 cells/uL). Additionally, labelling for miglustat (Zavesca) lists thrombocytopenia as a known risk with its use. All subjects in Trial IB1001-301 with laboratory values consistent with thrombocytopenia received concomitant miglustat therapy.
L653: During Treatment Period I, there was an equal number of subjects with platelets &lt;140 \times 10^3 cells/uL in those randomized to levacetylleucine (13/30, 43.3%) as those randomized to placebo (13/30, 43.3%). A similar proportion of subjects also had platelets &lt;125 \times 10^3 cells/uL (7/30 subjects randomized to levacetylleucine and 6/30 subjects randomized to placebo). There were four subjects randomized to levacetylleucine who had platelets &lt;100 \times 10^3 cells/uL, compared to two subjects randomized to placebo.
L654: For these four subjects with platelets &lt;100 \times 10^3 cells/uL, two had baseline values of &lt;100 \times 10^3 cells/uL that then fluctuated throughout the course of Treatment Period I (on levacetylleucine) and Treatment Period II (on placebo), ranging from 53 to 112 \times 10^3 cells/uL, as detailed in Table 38. There were no values &lt;50 \times 10^3 cells/uL. Two subjects had baseline values &gt;100 \times 10^3 cells/uL that decreased during the treatment period to 81 and 60 \times 10^3 cells/uL, respectively. However, in both instances, their platelet levels increased again to &gt;100 \times 10^3 cells/uL during Treatment Period II.

L658: While there are no symptoms typically associated with thrombocytopenia with platelets &gt;50 \times 10^3 cells/uL because patients with NPC are at risk for thrombocytopenia as part of their underlying disease and miglustat use is common, the potential for any additional risk associated with levacetylleucine may be clinically important. These laboratory findings will be included in labeling.

L691: ## 7.6.1.10. Open-Label Extension, Trial IB1001-301
L692: Eligible subjects who completed Trial IB1001-301 had the option to enroll in the ongoing OLE (IB1001-301 OLE) for up to 2 years of treatment with levacetylleucine. A total of 54 subjects entered the EP. The median duration of exposure at the 120-day safety update cut-off was 290 days (62 to 355). Safety information reported for the OLE consists primarily of summary data because the trial is ongoing.
L693: There have been no deaths reported in the OLE period thus far. There were five SAEs that occurred in three subjects, none of which were assessed as related to study drug.
L694: One subject was withdrawn after an SAE reported as multi-organ dysfunction due to inability to participate in future trial visits.
L695: ## 7.6.1.10.1. Serious Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301 OLE
L696: Three subjects had a total of five SAEs during the OLE. This trial is ongoing and the available details for each event provided by the Applicant are summarized below.
L697: ## Multi-Organ Dysfunction
L698: Subject (b) (6) was an adult male at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301. On Study Day 269 of the OLE, the subject was hospitalized due to multi-organ failure in the setting of an influenza A infection. The subject developed bilateral multifocal pneumonia which led to cardiorespiratory failure requiring cardiopulmonary resuscitation and assisted ventilation. Their hospitalization was also complicated by bacterial superinfection and septic shock, acute perimycarditis, pulmonary edema, and acute renal failure requiring intermittent hemodialysis. The subject had a tracheostomy and gastrostomy tube placed and a brain computerized tomography scan revealed brain atrophy. The subject was ultimately withdrawn by the clinical investigator at their trial site due to inability to participate in future trial visits and was placed on hospice care. The study drug was stopped at the time of his hospitalization and not restarted thereafter. The SAE of multi-organ dysfunction was assessed as unrelated to the study drug. This reviewer agrees that multi-organ dysfunction was related to the complications of influenza A infection with pneumonia and not likely related to the study drug.
L699: ## Urinary Tract Infection, Mycoplasma Pneumonia
L700: Subject (b) (6) was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301. The subject has a significant medical history of a previous liver transplant in addition to their diagnosis of NPC with recurrent gastrointestinal hemorrhage, an enlarged spleen, platelet dysfunction, recurrent diarrhea, and epilepsy. The subject was taking miglustat, ergocalciferol, sodium valproate, tranexamic acid, and tacrolimus in addition to levacetylleucine during the trial. Six weeks into the OLE, the subject was hospitalized for a urinary tract infection that was treated with antibiotics and resolved. No changes to the study drug were made during this hospitalization.
L701: Approximately 9 months into the OLE (during the first year), the subject was hospitalized with mycoplasma pneumonia confirmed via chest X-ray and a polymerase chain reaction test for mycoplasma. The subject was treated with antibiotics first in the hospital and then with an
L702: NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L703: outpatient course. The study drug was interrupted during his hospitalization for 3 days and then restarted at the same dose.
L704: Both SAEs were assessed as not related to the study drug. This reviewer agrees that both the urinary tract infection and the mycoplasma pneumonia are more likely to be related to the subject's underlying disease and not likely related to the study drug.
L705: ## Seizure
L706: Subject (b) (6) was an elementary school age female at the time of enrollment in IB1001-301. At the time of enrollment, the subject's relevant past medical history in addition to their diagnosis of NPC included delayed motor skills, impaired expressive language, ataxia, vertical gaze palsy, and splenomegaly. The subject was taking miglustat in addition to levacetylleucine during the trial. After approximately 9 months of open-label treatment with levacetylleucine, the subject experienced new onset seizures described as "stereotypical movements of the arm with grimacing and was not responsive to her parents." An electroencephalogram confirmed seizures with focal onset. A magnetic resonance image of the subject's brain was completed and did not note any specific findings related to her new onset seizures. The subject was hospitalized for 2 days to complete a work-up of new-onset seizures and started on levetiracetam, after which the AE of "seizure" was considered resolved. No changes to the study drug were made.
L707: Approximately 1 month after her initial diagnosis of epilepsy as detailed above (i.e., after about 10 months of open-label treatment with levacetylleucine), the subject was hospitalized for management of breakthrough seizures on levetiracetam. The subject was started on lamotrigine and discharged home after 1 day. No changes were made to the study drug.
L708: The Applicant and investigator assessed both events of seizure as unrelated to the study drug. In the setting of the subject's diagnosis of NPC and progressive neurological symptoms as well as them taking the study drug for &gt;9 months at the time of their initial diagnosis of epilepsy, this reviewer agrees that the subject's seizures are more likely due to progression of NPC and less likely due to levacetylleucine, although because this was a new diagnosis of epilepsy it is not possible to make definitive conclusions.
L709: ## 7.6.1.10.2. Common Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-301
## OLE
L710: Forty-three percent of subjects in the OLE experienced any treatment AE during the OLE. Like the parent study, the most common system organ classes were infections and infestations (16.7%) followed by gastrointestinal disorders (13%). Most reported AEs (preferred terms) occurred just once except for COVID-19 (n=3) and fall (n=2). The recurrence of COVID-19 diagnoses is not unexpected given the incidence in the general population during this time-period following the worldwide pandemic.
L711: AEs that are also ARs for the concomitant medication miglustat were gastrointestinal disorders and weight loss. Rash (reported with Tanganil use, which uses a wheat starch excipient) occurred in two subjects (eczema and rash erythematous).
L712: Data were presented in summary form only for this ongoing trial. Overall, the adverse events reported in the OLE period were similar to those reported during the parent study and did not raise any specific new safety concerns.

L717: ## 7.6.2. Safety Results, Trial IB1001-201
L718: ## Overall Summary
L719: Trial IB1001-201 was an open-label treatment study, thus it did not have a placebo-control group and was not the primary focus of our safety assessment. A total of 33 subjects with NPC between the ages of 7 and 64 years were enrolled, of which 31 completed treatment with levacetylleucine for 6 weeks followed by a 6-week washout period during the parent study. AEs that occurred in two subjects or more while on treatment with levacetylleucine were diarrhea, seizures, epistaxis, fall, rash, rhinitis, and upper respiratory tract infections. All these AEs were also reported in at least one subject during the placebo-controlled Trial IB1001-301 and remained relatively infrequent, thus no new specific treatment related risks were identified.
L720: There were no SAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There were no deaths during the parent study. There was one withdrawal due to worsening epilepsy that was reported as mild in intensity and assessed as not related to the study drug.
L721: ## 7.6.2.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
## Summary, Trial IB1001-201
L722: Table 43 provides a summary of AEs reported during the parent study, Trial IB1001-201. One or more TEAEs were reported by more subjects exposed to levacetylleucine (IB1001) than during the washout period (70% versus 21%). In the overall cohort, there were six SAEs in four subjects and no deaths.

L743: ## Upper Respiratory Tract Infection
L744: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment Trial IB1001-201. The subject was hospitalized on Study Day 14 for an upper respiratory infection, which resolved on Study Day 16. The study drug was interrupted briefly during the hospitalization and restarted. This event was assessed as not related to the study medication by the Applicant. This reviewer agrees that the upper respiratory tract infection was likely related to his underlying disease, not the study drug.
L745: ## **Lower Respiratory Tract Infection**
L746: Subject (b) (6) was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The subject's past medical history was relevant for prior gastrostomy tube placement, sialorrhea, epilepsy, cataplexy, and recurrent respiratory infection in addition to their diagnosis of NPC. The subject was hospitalized on Study Day 30 for a lower respiratory tract infection that required intravenous antibiotics and resolved on Study Day 39. The study drug was continued throughout the hospitalization. This event was assessed as not related to the study medication by the clinical investigator. This reviewer agrees that the AE of lower respiratory tract infection is likely related to progression of NPC, sialorrhea, and recurrent lung infection.
L747: ## **Dehydration**
L748: Subject (b) (6) was an elementary school age male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The subject's relevant past medical history besides a diagnosis of NPC is significant for a liver transplant, chronic gastrointestinal inflammation, recurrent gastrointestinal hemorrhage, and anemia. On Study Day 23, the subject developed a "chest" infection treated with amoxicillin and was hospitalized for dehydration for 24 hours on Study Day 26. The study drug was interrupted during the hospital stay only and restarted at the same dose. The SAE of dehydration was assessed as unrelated to the study drug and attributed to amoxicillin by the clinical investigator. It is difficult to determine whether amoxicillin was the culprit based on the information provided; however, this reviewer agrees that the hospitalization was likely related to either the infection or the associated treatment, not the study drug.
L749: ## **Fall With Head Injury and Rib Fracture**
L750: Subject (b) (6) was an adult female at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The subject had a history of previous recurrent falls. The subject had a fall that resulted in a closed head injury and multiple rib fractures resulting in a hospitalization from Study Day 44 to Study Day 50. The study drug was interrupted for 6 days during the hospitalization. The event was reported as resolved 1 month later. This SAE was assessed as not related to the study drug because there were no features suggesting a drug-related cause (e.g., new onset hypotension). This reviewer agrees that a traumatic fall was unlikely related to the study drug and more likely related to symptoms of ataxia or abnormal gait due to NPC or an unrelated accidental fall.
L751: ## **7.6.2.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation, Trial IB1001-201**
L752: One subject discontinued the trial due to worsening epilepsy (seizures increased).
L753: Subject (b) (6) was an adolescent male at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. Relevant medical history besides a diagnosis of NPC was epilepsy requiring three antiseizure medications (ethosuximide, valproate, and clobazam). The subject had additional seizures reported as "mild" on Study Day 10. The subject was treated with three additional antiseizure medications with some improvement but was ultimately withdrawn from the trial at the discretion of the clinical investigator on Study Day 36. This reviewer agrees that the increase in seizures of mild intensity was unlikely to be related to the study drug and more likely related to progression of NPC with treatment-refractory epilepsy.
L754: ## 7.6.2.5. Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events, Trial IB1001-201
L755: Overall, 73% of subjects experienced a TEAE while on treatment with levacetylleucine versus 21% during the washout period.

L764: During treatment with levacetylleucine, the AEs that occurred most were diarrhea and seizure in three subjects, followed by fall, lower respiratory tract infection, rash pruritic, rhinitis, and upper respiratory infection, which occurred in two subjects each. Diarrhea, seizure, fall and rhinitis all occurred in one subject each during the washout period as well. All other AEs occurred in one subject each. These TEAEs are generally similar to those seen in Trial IB1001-301. Because the parent study of Trial IB1001-201 was both an open-label study and of shorter duration than the pivotal trial, it would be challenging to determine whether any of the AEs that occurred infrequently during Trial IB1001-201 and only once during Trial IB1001-301 are more likely to be attributable to levacetylleucine rather than NPC or concomitant medications. Thus, labeling will reflect the AEs identified during the double-blind placebo-controlled trial during Treatment Period I.
L765: ## 7.6.2.6. Laboratory Findings, Trial IB1001-201
L766: Routine safety monitoring bloodwork (hematology, clinical chemistry, creatine kinase, liver function analyses) and urinalysis were obtained. The mean values for most of these tests remained within normal ranges except for platelet levels, which were in the range of thrombocytopenia both during treatment with levacetylleucine and during the washout period.
L767: ## **Hematology**
L768: Table 46 demonstrates abnormalities in hematology laboratory values during Trial IB1001-201. Most of the hematology laboratory values were within normal limits except for platelet levels, which revealed thrombocytopenia (&lt;150 \times 10^3 cells/uL) both during open-label treatment with levacetylleucine and during the washout period.
