L776: ## **Thrombocytopenia**
L777: There was a similar number of subjects during the treatment and washout periods with thrombocytopenia. During the treatment period, 14/33 (42%) subjects had platelets &lt;140 103 cells/uL compared to 12/31 (39%) during the washout period. There were 8/33 (24%) compared to 7/31 (23%) with platelets &lt;125 103 cells/uL, and 2 subjects during the treatment period and 1 subject during the washout period with platelets &lt;100 103 cells/uL. Because patients with NPC can have thrombocytopenia and because thrombocytopenia is also a known risk of miglustat, it is not clear if there is an increased risk of thrombocytopenia attributable to levacetylleucine from this data. Because this risk was similarly demonstrated in Trial IB1001-301, these results from Trial IB101-201 did not raise additional safety concerns.
L778: ## **7.6.2.7. Open-Label Extension, Trial IB1001-201**
L779: Eligible subjects who completed Trial IB1001-201 had the option to enroll in an OLE (IB1001-201 OLE) for up to 2 years of treatment with levacetylleucine. A total of 19 subjects entered the EP, of which 13 subjects completed the trial. There were two open-label treatment periods, Treatment Period I consisted of 1 year of open-label treatment, followed by a 6-week washout period, and then Treatment Period II, which consisted of a second year of open-label treatment. The total duration of exposure during the OLE ranged from 176 to 816 days.
L780: There were no deaths reported in the OLE. There were 22 SAEs reported in five subjects, none of which were assessed as related to study drug. There were no AEs that lead to trial withdrawal. Of note, the EP encompassed the beginning and much of the duration of the COVID-19 pandemic, during which there were worldwide restrictions on travel and heightened health concerns, especially for medically complex individuals. Twelve subjects who had completed IB1001-201 did not enroll in the OLE, and six subjects withdrew consent from the OLE without a clear explanation, which the Agency suspects may have been impacted by the COVID-19 pandemic, as noted in Section 6.2.1.2.
L781: ## 7.6.2.7.1. Serious Adverse Events, IB1001-201 Open-Label Extension
L782: Five subjects had a total of 22 SAEs during the OLE. There were three subjects who experienced aspiration pneumonia, otherwise each SAE was unique and included the following: gastrointestinal hemorrhage and melena, seizure, a uterine leiomyoma requiring hysterectomy, gastric device dislocation causing gastric perforation, cataplexy, and viral pneumonia. Most of the SAEs reported occurred in one subject who had multiple complications of NPC during the OLE phase, as detailed below. All SAEs were assessed as not related to the study drug by the Applicant.

L784: Subject (b) (6) was an adult female at the time of enrollment in Trial IB1001-201. The subject had a history of dysphagia and was hospitalized for aspiration pneumonia on Study Day 468 until Study Day 482. The subject continued the study drug during their hospitalization. The AE of aspiration pneumonia was assessed as not related to the study drug. This reviewer agrees that this event was more likely related to NPC and/or dysphagia and not likely related to the study drug.

L811: Treatment-emergent adverse events defined as any AEs that appeared during or after study treatment and was absent before, or an AE which was present before treatment and worsened while on treatment.
L812: Duration is 1 year in first extension period, 1 year in second extension period.
L813: Coded as MedDRA preferred terms.

L815: The most common AEs that occurred during either of the 1-year-long open-label treatment periods included falls (nine subjects), coronavirus infection (four subjects), contusion and aspiration pneumonia (three subjects), and dysphagia (two subjects). There were more falls reported during the first year of open-label treatment (8) compared to the second year of open-label treatment (2). Because there is no control group for comparison, conclusions regarding increased frequency of falls with prolonged treatment could not be drawn.
L816: Generally, the AEs reported during both the parent study and OLE of Trial IB1001-201 were similar to those reported during Trial IB1001-301 with the exception of multiple coronavirus infections, which reflects the timing of the trial during the coronavirus pandemic. There were no additional new safety concerns raised during the OLE of Trial IB1001-201.
L817: ## 7.6.3. Safety Results, Other Indications
L818: ## 7.6.3.1. Trial IB1001-202
L819: ## Summary
L820: Trial IB1001-202, an open-label treatment study in GM2 gangliosidosis, was reviewed only for SAEs, withdrawals, and deaths. GM2 gangliosidosis is another autosomal recessive lysosomal storage disorder with some overlapping signs and symptoms seen in NPC.
L821: In general, levacetylleucine was well-tolerated in Trial IB1001-202 and there were no SAEs, AEs leading to withdrawal from the trial, or deaths that raised any new safety concerns in our review of levacetylleucine.
L822: ## Trial IB1001-202, Parent Study
L823: A total of 30 subjects between the ages of 6 and 55 years were enrolled, of which 27 subjects completed treatment with levacetylleucine for 6 weeks followed by a 6-week washout period during the parent study. The duration of levacetylleucine exposure in the parent study ranged from 16 to 132 days.
L824: There were four SAEs that occurred in two subjects, none of which were assessed as related to the study drug. There were no deaths during the parent study. There was one withdrawal after 16 days of treatment with levacetylleucine for tremor without any further follow-up for an assessment and determination of causation, and one withdrawal due to COVID-19 pandemic-related travel restrictions.
L825: SAEs that occurred during Trial IB1001-201 include a fall causing a vertebral fracture in one subject and an exacerbation of previously diagnosed bipolar disorder with psychotic features in another subject; neither of these SAEs were assessed as related to the study drug.
L826: ## IB1001-202 Open-Label Extension
L827: Subjects who completed the parent study were eligible to enroll in an open-label extension study for up to 2 additional years of open-label treatment with a 6-week washout period in between the two open-label treatment periods. Fourteen subjects enrolled in the EP, and just seven subjects completed both years of treatment. The duration of levacetylleucine exposure during the OLE ranged from 266 to 754 days.
L828: Like Trial IB1001-201, this trial coincided with the emergence of the COVID-19 pandemic, and there were multiple withdrawals from the trial with limited details regarding the reason for the withdrawal.
L829: There was one SAE reported in one subject during the OLE. This SAE was an exacerbation of bipolar disorder that occurred during the washout period of the OLE and assessed as not related to the study drug. There were no TEAEs that led to withdrawal from the trial. There were no deaths.
L830: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L831: ## 7.7.1. Embryofetal Development Toxicity in Rodents and Non-Rodents
L832: ## Issue
L833: Dose-range finding animal embryo-fetal development (EFD) toxicity studies in rats and rabbits demonstrate EFD toxicity including induction of malformations following administration of levacetylleucine during pregnancy. However, final study reports for EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits have not been submitted. Additionally, because the protocols for EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits were not submitted to the agency, it is uncertain if the doses that were selected for definitive studies were adequate to evaluate the risk to EFD.
L834: ## Background
L835: In pregnant rats, increases in resorptions, post-implantation loss, and skeletal malformation were seen due to exposure to levacetylleucine at doses of 600 mg/kg/day and at the human-equivalent dose of 97 mg/kg/day. In pregnant rabbits, increased resorptions, post-implantation loss, decreased fetal body weight, and increased skeletal malformations were observed following exposure to levacetylleucine at doses of 1250 mg/kg/day and at the human-equivalent dose of 403 mg/kg/day.
L836: ## Assessment
L837: Concern for a CS risk to EFD was demonstrated in animal studies. Specifically, nonclinical effects occurred in both nonclinical species. A Warning and Precaution for EFD toxicity that describes the potential risk to a fetus with recommendations to mitigate the risk (i.e., effective contraception) should be included in labeling. In addition, information was also described in Section 8.1 *Pregnancy* and in Section 8.3 *Females and Males of Reproductive Potential* subsections of the label (Table 89). See Section 8.4 of this review for details.
L838: ## Conclusion
L839: A Warning and Precaution for EFD toxicity recommends that females of reproductive potential avoid pregnancy and use effective contraception during treatment with levacetylleucine. Submission of final study reports for EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits will be required as a postmarketing requirement (PMR). Additionally, because it is uncertain if the doses and trial design for definitive studies were appropriate for reasons described above, the Applicant may be required to repeat the EFD toxicity studies in rats and rabbits after the dose selection and trial protocols have been reviewed by the Agency.
L840: ## 7.7.2. Juvenile Toxicology Profile Unknown
L841: ## Issue
L842: A juvenile toxicity study with levacetylleucine has not been conducted. Evaluation of toxicity of levacetylleucine in juvenile animals is needed because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and treatment with levacetylleucine may be lifelong. Data in the rat and rabbit EFD studies indicate levacetylleucine crossed the placenta and modulated growth of bones, and it is expected that levacetylleucine will modulate the growth of bones in juvenile animals as well. Because levacetylleucine crosses the blood brain barrier and because its major metabolite, leucine, causes defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck, and depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967), levacetylleucine has the potential to influence growth, neurological development (learning and memory), sexual development, and general health. Hence, there is need for a juvenile toxicity study to determine the toxic effects of levacetylleucine in juvenile animals and to inform patients of potential risks of this medication. These risks cannot be readily characterized in patients in the postmarketing setting.
L843: ## Conclusion
L844: While the current clinical safety and efficacy data appear to be adequate to support approval in pediatric patients, the available data are limited; therefore, a juvenile toxicity study will be required as a PMR.
L845: ## 7.7.3. Effects on Male and Female Fertility and Early Embryonic Development Unknown
L846: ## Issue
L847: Rodent male and female fertility and early embryonic development toxicity studies with levacetylleucine has not been conducted. Evaluation of effects of levacetylleucine on fertility and early embryonic development is needed because there are adult-onset forms of NPC and because treatment with levacetylleucine for early onset NPC may continue into reproductive age. As such, some patients of reproductive age are likely to be exposed. Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid that appears to have widespread distribution; therefore, placental transfer is expected, and there may be an impact on embryo implantation, as suggested by data from the non-good laboratory practice dose-ranging embryofetal development studies that were submitted in the NDA. Levacetylleucine appeared to reduce the number of implantations in dams treated in an EFD study; therefore, the purpose of this study is to evaluate whether levacetylleucine may affect fertility through inhibition of implantation or through other effects on gametogenesis.
L848: ## Conclusion
L849: Information was described in the impairment of fertility subsection of labeling (Table 89; Section 13.1) to indicate that studies of fertility and early embryonic development and pre- and postnatal toxicology were not conducted with the drug. These risks cannot be readily characterized in patients. Rodent male and female fertility and early embryonic development toxicity studies will be required as PMRs to support risk communication to patients and caregivers.
L850: ## 7.7.4. Effects on Pre- and Postnatal Development Unknown
L851: ## Issue
L852: A rodent pre- and postnatal development toxicity study with levacetylleucine has not been conducted. Evaluation of the effects of levacetylleucine on pre- and postnatal development is needed because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and treatment with levacetylleucine may begin in infancy and be lifelong. Levacetylleucine was found to cause increases in post-implantation loss and malformation in skeletal bones in a dose-range finding EFD study in rabbits. Leucine, a metabolite of levacetylleucine, as a single 0.2 mL injection of a 1% solution in the amnionic sac of a nine-day-old chicken embryo is reported to cause morphological anomalies of the legs (including inward flexion of the toes, leg extension or flexion at hip joint), defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck, and depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967). Therefore, exposure to levacetylleucine has the potential to cause adverse effects on growth and development (neurological, physical, and sexual) from prenatal and into postnatal stages of life. This possibility requires investigation. Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid that appears to have widespread distribution; therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected. Significant exposure to a fetus and/or neonate is likely. This study will evaluate the effect of the drug on growth, maturation, and reproduction when offspring are exposed during pregnancy and lactation. This risk cannot be readily characterized in patients.
L853: ## Conclusion
L854: Information was described in the impairment of fertility subsection of labeling (Table 89; Section 8.1) to indicate that study of pre- and postnatal toxicology has not been conducted. A rodent pre- and postnatal development toxicity study will be required as a PMR.
L855: ## 7.7.5. Carcinogenic Risk Unknown
L856: ## Issue
L857: Carcinogenicity studies with levacetylleucine have not been conducted. Leucine, a metabolite of levacetylleucine, is known to activate mammalian target of rapamycin (mTOR) signaling and promotes pancreatic tumor growth in mice (Liu et al. 2014). Due to the role of leucine in promotion of tumors, it is likely that exposure to levacetylleucine and to leucine as a metabolite may increase carcinogenic risk in humans. Levacetylleucine is intended for lifetime administration beginning in infancy or childhood. Because potential neoplastic effects may not manifest in patients for many years, the risk cannot be evaluated in a timely manner in humans. Therefore, carcinogenicity studies are needed to determine potential carcinogenicity risk to patients.
L858: ## Conclusion
L859: Information was described in the carcinogenesis subsection of labeling to indicate that animal carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with the drug. A 26-week carcinogenicity study in the transgenic rasH2 mouse will be required as a PMR, along with a 2-year rat carcinogenicity study. Alternatively, the Agency may accept a weight-of-evidence evaluation in lieu of conducting the study if the Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee agrees that the study will not add value. However, to date, the Applicant has not submitted a justification for a weight-of-evidence evaluation.
L860: ## 7.7.6. Concomitant Miglustat Use
L861: ## Issue
L862: The high degree of concomitant miglustat use in Trial IB1001-301 impacts the determination of risk for levacetylleucine.
L863: ## Background
L864: Miglustat is considered standard of care for patients with NPC by treating clinicians. Miglustat is approved for the progressive neurological symptoms of NPC in other countries and is used off-label in the United States. The percentage of patients with concomitant use of miglustat in Trial IB1001-301 was 87%.
L865: The current labeling for miglustat contains 'Warnings and Precautions' for the following: tremor, gastrointestinal events such as diarrhea and weight loss, peripheral neuropathy, and thrombocytopenia. Similar findings, particularly of gastrointestinal symptoms, were identified in IB1001-301, making interpretation of AEs strictly related to levacetylleucine challenging.
L866: ## Assessment
L867: The assessment of the safety of levacetylleucine in patients diagnosed with NPC is based on the comparison to placebo. However, the current standard of care in this multinational trial includes the use of miglustat, which was used by most trial participants in both treatment arms at their baseline dose during the trial. AEs that occurred in at least two subjects or more while on treatment with levacetylleucine in the placebo-controlled parent Trial IB1001-301 include the following: abdominal pain, dysphagia, upper respiratory infections, and vomiting.
L868: Labeling will reflect these AEs. Several of these AEs overlap with those currently listed on the miglustat label, so it is difficult to determine if these events were attributable to levacetylleucine.
L869: ## Conclusion
L870: As the number of enrolled subjects not treated with concomitant miglustat is low, it is difficult to make a comparison of those receiving levacetylleucine with or without miglustat. The data provide an assessment of levacetylleucine, with most subjects on background miglustat. While there are limitations in attributing certain AEs to either miglustat or levacetylleucine (which appeared to have some similarities in safety profile), the data are adequate to support the safety of levacetylleucine with and without background miglustat use.
L871: ## 7.7.7. Assessment of Safety in Patients &lt;10 Years Old (\ge 15 kg)
L872: ## Issue/Background
L873: Trial IB1001-301, the only placebo-controlled trial in the levacetylleucine development program, enrolled seven (11.7%) subjects who were &lt;10 years old; the youngest subject was 5 years old. There were two (6.1%) subjects &lt;10 years old in the open-label Trial IB1001-201, and six (20%) subjects &lt;10 years old in the open-label study for another indication (GM2 gangliosidosis). The levacetylleucine development program did not include any subjects who were &lt;5 years old for NPC or any other indication. The inclusion criteria for
L874: Trial IB1001-301 specified that subjects should weigh \ge 15 kg; however, there were no subjects who weighed &lt;20 kg enrolled in the trial.
L875: ## Assessment
L876: During the review cycle the Applicant provided real world data from 17 subjects who were under the age of 5 years old (ranging from 4 months to 3 years old) and who had received Tanganil (N-acetyl-DL-leucine) as a component of their clinical care as reported to the Applicant by Dr. Michael Strupp, a neurologist in Munich, Germany. Just three of these subjects had a diagnosis of NPC; the remainder had other genetic diagnoses including Tay-Sachs disease, Sandhoff disease (GM2 gangliosidoses), and Ataxia-Telangiectasia. Dosages ranged from 1 gram per day to 2.5 grams per day, divided into BID or TID doses. Treatment durations ranges from 2 to 73 months. The three subjects with NPC were 18 months, 2 years, and 3 years old at the time of treatment initiation. All three were reported to have been on treatment with Tanganil for 12 months or more (range: 12 to 73 months). There were no drug-related AEs reported, although notably these subjects are not being monitored in a clinical trial during which AEs would be systematically reported. No PK values were provided.
L877: Anecdotal reports submitted by subject/caregivers to an online registry were also provided. All these subjects had a diagnosis of GM2 gangliosidosis, were between the ages of 8 months and 4 years old, and the only AEs noted were gastrointestinal upset or potential gastrointestinal reflux; most reported no side effects.
L878: The Applicant is not aware of any real-world data for individuals &lt;5 years old who have taken levacetylleucine (as opposed to the racemate).(b) (4)
L879: For subjects in the placebo-controlled pivotal Trial IB1001-301, there were no serious safety signals. Subjects &lt;10 years old did not experience significantly more or different AEs than subjects \ge 10 years old.
L880: Based on the PopPK analyses (see Section 6.3.2), the exposures following the proposed dose in subjects &lt;10 years old and weighing \ge 15 kg are expected to be similar to the exposures in subjects \ge 10 years.
L881: ## Conclusion
L882: Levacetylleucine is expected to be well-tolerated at the to-be-marketed doses for individuals &lt;10 years old and weighing \ge 15 kg. There are limited clinical safety data for subjects &lt;10 years old, and no data for levacetylleucine in subjects &lt;5 years old or &lt;20 kg. However, important considerations are the relatively benign safety profile of levacetylleucine overall in the available clinical trials, anecdotal reports of the to-be-marketed or higher doses in subjects &lt;4 years old with the related product Tanganil, as well the similar PK profile following the to-be marketed dose in subjects &gt;10 years old compared to the subjects \ge 10 years old. These data are considered sufficient to support a limited amount of extrapolation of safety to support use in pediatric patients weighing \ge 15 kg. Further considerations included the favorable benefit/risk profile, that NPC is a genetic disease with the same pathophysiology across the age spectrum, that while there can be heterogeneity in manifestations, there was similarity in response to treatment in terms of safety across the studied populations, and that the drug is not anticipated to behave differently in slightly younger children. Thus, it is anticipated that there will be similarity in the safety profile across the studied pediatric subjects who were \ge 5 years old and younger patients weighing
L883: \ge 15 kg. Routine pharmacovigilance will be adequate to continue assessment of an acceptable risk profile in the approved population.
L884: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L885: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L886: The recommended dosage regimen of levacetylleucine in patients with NPC is based on body weight, which is the approach used in the clinical trials of levacetylleucine. Body weight was identified as a significant covariate affecting levacetylleucine PK in the PopPK analyses. The currently available data do not support a need for further therapeutic individualization based on other intrinsic factors.
L887: ## Body Weight
L888: Body weight was identified as a significant covariate on apparent clearance and apparent volume of distribution in the PopPK analysis (see Section 14.5). The apparent clearance and apparent volume of distribution of levacetylleucine increases with increasing body weight. These effects on PK of levacetylleucine are minimized by the recommended weight-based dosing regimen.
L889: ## Sex
L890: Sex was not identified as a significant covariate in the PopPK analysis, and therefore, not expected to have CS effect on levacetylleucine exposures.
L891: ## Subjects With NPC vs. Healthy Subjects
L892: PK data were not available for healthy subjects.
L893: ## Hepatic Impairment or Renal Impairment
L894: The effects of renal impairment and hepatic impairment on the PK of levacetylleucine have not been studied. No mass balance study has been conducted with levacetylleucine in humans. The metabolism of levacetylleucine results in the formation of acetate and L-leucine, and both byproducts are used within the body in further catabolic pathways. One study demonstrated that after an intravenous dose of ^{14}\text{C}-L-leucine to rats, 5% of the substrate was excreted in urine as levacetylleucine (Lee et al. 2015). Thus, the impact of hepatic impairment on PK of levacetylleucine is expected to be low.
L895: Patients with NPC with later onset primarily present with neurological symptoms. While some patients with NPC may exhibit liver organomegaly, significant liver impairment is generally not observed in this disease (Geberhiwot et al. 2018). Trials IB1001-201 and IB1001-301 did not include specific inclusion/exclusion criteria for liver function. In these trials, the liver function status of subjects (i.e., normal, mild, moderate, or severe) was not explicitly assessed using tools such as the Child-Pugh classification, Mayo risk score, or a comprehensive set of hepatic function markers. However, based on bilirubin levels, which is a key indicator of liver function, as well as other liver blood chemistry parameters (AST, ALT, alkaline phosphatase) monitored throughout the trials, the subjects generally appeared to have normal liver function. This absence of specific liver function assessments limits the ability to correlate liver function status with PK parameters.
L896: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L897: ## Drug-Drug Interaction
L898: Levacetylleucine was found to not inhibit cytochrome P450 (CYP) enzymes or induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 enzymes via in vitro studies, indicating a low risk for drug-drug interactions through these pathways. Although levacetylleucine is identified as a substrate of organic anion transporter 1 and OAT3, these interactions are not considered clinically relevant due to the established safety profile and minimal renal excretion of levacetylleucine. Breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) - and P-glycoprotein (P-gp)-mediated levacetylleucine active transport was not detected. In addition, the Applicant assessed the potential for levacetylleucine to inhibit efflux transporters (e.g., P-gp, BCRP, and bile salt export pump; see Section 14). The risk associated with bile salt export pump inhibition is low due to the C_{max}/IC_{50} of &lt;0.1. The risk of drug-drug interactions due to BCRP inhibition is also considered low, as the I_{gut}/IC_{50} is slightly higher than the accepted ratio of 10. However, the potential for P-gp inhibition in vivo cannot be ruled out, given the significantly elevated I_{gut}/IC_{50}. Consequently, the review team recommends more frequent monitoring for ARs related to P-gp substrates in the label, as well as a PMR trial to further evaluate the potential interactions with P-gp substrates.
L899: ## Food Effect
L900: The effect of food on PK of the to-be-marketed formulation was not assessed in a dedicated clinical trial. Based on the clinical trial protocols, levacetylleucine was supposed to be administered to subjects with NPC without food (defined as the drug needing to be taken either 30 minutes before a meal or at least 2 hours after a meal). However, approximately 12.5% non-compliance incidents (subjects took the drug within 2 hours after a meal) were reported in the clinical trials. The review team conducted a preliminary PK analysis to compare the drug concentration during the first 2.5 hours postdose under “fasted” (subjects took the drug following the protocols regarding food intake) and fed (subjects took the drug non-compliant with the protocols regarding food intake) conditions (see Section 14). Based on this assessment, similar PK during the absorption phase was observed regardless of food intake. However, due to the small sample size of subjects who had intensive PK data that enabled this analysis and uncontrolled food conditions (i.e., high fat or low fat), these findings cannot support a definitive conclusion. Because patients with NPC often experience feeding difficulties and may require a long time to complete meals, enforcing the administration of the drug without food for a TID dosing regimen would be challenging and burdensome for these patients (i.e., patients will need to fast for 2.5 hours). Therefore, based on the preliminary PK analysis and practical considerations, the review team recommends the drug to be taken with or without food in the label. To address the uncertainty associated with the impact of food on PK, a postmarketing commitment trial has been proposed.
L901: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L902: Under the Pediatric Research Equity Act (21 U.S.C. 355c), all applications for new active ingredients (which includes new salts and new fixed combinations), new indications, new dosage forms, new dosing regimens, or new routes of administration are required to contain an assessment of the safety and effectiveness of the product for the claimed indication in pediatric patients unless this requirement is waived, deferred, or inapplicable.
L903: NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L904: Because this drug product for this indication has an orphan drug designation, it is exempt from this requirement.
L905: The currently available data support the approval of this product in patients weighing \ge 15 kg.
L906: ## Nonclinical
L907: There are no nonclinical data to support the safety of levacetylleucine in children; however, available clinical data suggest that the drug is adequately tolerated. A juvenile animal study will be conducted as a PMR to assess target organ effects and effects on developmental processes including neurocognitive development, growth, and sexual maturation.
L908: ## Clinical
L909: The safety and effectiveness of levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC have been established in 23 pediatric subjects weighing \ge 15 kg in Trial IB1001-301. Use of levacetylleucine for this indication is supported by evidence from one adequate and well-controlled trial in adult and pediatric subjects weighing \ge 15 kg with additional pharmacokinetic data from 40 adult and 17 pediatric subjects who participated in two open-label studies. The safety and effectiveness of levacetylleucine have not been established in pediatric subjects weighing &lt;15 kg. See Section 7.7.7 for additional information regarding the safety assessment of pediatric subjects weighing \ge 15 kg.
L910: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L911: ## Animal Data
L912: In a dose-ranging rat EFD study, doses of up to 1000 mg/kg/day oral levacetylleucine were administered to pregnant females during organogenesis (gestation day [GD] 6 through GD 17). Increases in the mean number of resorptions, mean post-implantation losses, and skeletal malformations (thoracic arch of the vertebra-fused thoracic arch, misaligned thoracic arch, hemicentric thoracic centrum, and misaligned thoracic centrum; vertebral column-scoliosis, and ribs-absent costal cartilage and short rib) were observed at doses \ge 600 mg/kg/day, which is 1.4-fold the MRHD of IB1001 (levacetylleucine) based on body surface area.
L913: In a dose-ranging rabbit EFD study, doses of up to 2500 mg/kg/day oral levacetylleucine were administered daily to pregnant females during organogenesis (GD 7 through GD 19). Increased embryo-fetal death (post-implantation loss/resorption), decrease in fetal body weight, and increase in external (open or partially open eyes, hyperflexion of limbs) and skeletal malformations (misshapen maxilla and premaxilla, and small interparietal bones) were observed at the \ge 1250 mg/kg/day, which is 6-fold the MRHD of levacetylleucine based on body surface area.
L914: ## 9. Product Quality
L915: ## Drug Substance
L916: Levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid; it is acetyl amide of the amino acid L-leucine. Levacetylleucine has not been previously approved or marketed in the United States, and therefore, it is classified as a new molecular entity.
L917: ## NDA 219132
## Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L918: Levacetylleucine is a white crystalline powder with a melting point of 187.6°C and specific optical rotation [\alpha]_{D}^{25}=-23^{\circ}. It is slightly hygroscopic. Levacetylleucine is slightly soluble in water and free soluble in dimethyl sulfoxide.
L919: Levacetylleucine has the chemical name 2-acetamido-4-methylpentanoic acid, the empirical formula C_8H_{15}NO_3, a molecular weight of 173.21 g/mol, and the chemical structure is as shown in Figure 13.
L920: **Figure 13. Chemical Structure for Levacetylleucine**
L921: Source: Structural characterization provided in drug substance module 3.2.S.3.1
L922: The chemical structure for this drug substance has been characterized and confirmed by multiple analytical techniques and provided in the module 3.2.S.3.1 of this NDA.
L923: Levacetylleucine for this application is manufactured in accordance with the current good manufacturing practices (cGMP) requirements. It is tested and release against a specification that assures the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug substance at release and throughout its proposed retest date of (b) (4) months. Sufficient stability data is provided in the application in support of the proposed retest date of (b) (4) months; the retest date is granted.
L924: ## **Drug Product**
L925: AQNEURSA® (levacetylleucine) for oral suspension, 1 gram is supplied as white to off-white strawberry flavored granules in a unit-dose packet. It is indicated for the treatment of adult and pediatric patients with neurological symptoms of NPC and who weigh \ge 15 kg.
L926: Each unit-dose packet contains 1.7 grams of white to off-white granules equivalent to 1 gram of levacetylleucine as the active ingredient. The granules also contain hypromellose, isomalt, and strawberry flavor as the inactive ingredients.
L927: AQNEURSA (levacetylleucine) for oral suspension is manufactured (b) (4) for IntraBio, Inc. in accordance with the cGMP requirements. It is tested and release against a specification that assures the identity, strength, purity, and quality of the drug product at release and throughout its proposed expiration dating period of 24 months. Sufficient stability data in support of the proposed expiration date is provided in the application and the expiration dating period of 24 months is granted to this drug product.
L928: AQNEURSA (levacetylleucine) for oral suspension is supplied in a carton containing 28 unit-dose packets. It is also supplied as a multipack containing four cartons providing 112 unit-dose packets. This drug product should be stored at 20°C to 25°C (68°F to 77°F); excursion permitted between 15°C to 30°C (59°F to 86°F) [see United States Pharmacopeia Controlled Room Temperature].

L944: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L945: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L946: The Applicant, IntraBio Inc, has investigated levacetylleucine, also referred to by the company development code IB1001 and by its chemical name N-acetyl-L-leucine (NALL), for its ability to treat Niemann-Pick disease type C (NPC). The Applicant's development program began with the investigation of commercially available racemic mixture N-acetyl-DL-leucine, a modified amino acid approved in France since 1957 as Tanganil (Pierre Fabre Laboratories) for short term (10 days to 6 weeks) treatment of acute vertigo in adults. The Applicant opened a pre-investigational new drug application (PIND) file (PIND 134369) and submitted a PIND meeting request on February 22, 2017, to discuss their development program for N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The meeting was granted by the Agency; however, the Applicant cancelled the meeting on April 13, 2017 because it determined the program needed additional refinement before any discussions should occur. The Applicant also received an orphan drug designation for N-acetyl-DL-leucine for the treatment of NPC on February 22, 2018.
L947: The Applicant resubmitted a PIND meeting request on February 26, 2018, this time to discuss their nonclinical and clinical plan to develop levacetylleucine, the L-enantiomer and active ingredient of the racemic mixture of N-acetyl-DL-leucine. The Applicant asserted that animal studies suggested that the L-enantiomer had a greater potential clinical benefit over the racemic mixture. The Applicant intended to formulate levacetylleucine as granules for oral suspension for the treatment of NPC (b) (4). In the PIND meeting, held on July 17, 2018, the FDA commented on the Applicant's proposed plan to conduct a phase 2/2b, open-label clinical trial to assess the safety and efficacy of levacetylleucine. The FDA asserted that an open-label trial could be used for proof-of-concept, but that it would be inadequate to generate interpretable efficacy data to support a future marketing application. General comments were also provided, particularly for nonclinical studies, primary endpoints considerations, the statistical analysis approach, and the clinical pharmacology development plan. In addition, the FDA advised the Applicant (b) (4). The meeting minutes were issued on July 26, 2018.
L948: On January 7, 2019, IND 134369 was submitted and included protocol IB1001-201 titled "Effects of N-Acetyl-L-Leucine on Niemann-Pick Disease Type C: A Multinational, Multicenter, Open-Label, Rater-Blinded Phase II Study." Per the FDA's request, a teleconference took place on January 23, 2019. In the teleconference, the FDA reiterated the concerns regarding the limitations of an open-label trial design versus a randomized, controlled trial and reminded the Applicant that Trial IB1001-201, as designed, would only provide short term safety information. The Sponsor acknowledged the FDA's concerns and stated that Trial IB101-201 was an exploratory study and agreed to revise the protocol per FDA's recommendation to eliminate the open-label extension and limit the trial to 6 weeks in duration. The FDA issued a Study May Proceed letter on February 7, 2019. The FDA recommended the Applicant request a guidance meeting to discuss improving the trial design (e.g., incorporating randomization) and to further discuss some of the trial's key elements (e.g., the clinical outcome assessment endpoints and statistical approach). General
L949: recommendations on the plan for clinical pharmacology studies were also provided in the letter.
L950: In their IND submission received by FDA on January 7, 2019, the Applicant also included a breakthrough therapy designation request for levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC. Following an evaluation of this request, the FDA issued a breakthrough therapy designation request denied letter on March 5, 2019.
L951: On January 31, 2020, the Applicant submitted a revised protocol for Trial IB1001-201. This resulted in another teleconference at the FDA's request, which was held on February 25, 2020. The teleconference was followed by an advice letter issued by the FDA on February 28, 2020. The FDA informed the Applicant that the revised protocol for Trial IB1001-201 was not sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness to support a marketing application, primarily due to its short treatment duration, open-label design, and subjective primary endpoint. The FDA again advised the Applicant to submit a meeting request to further discuss the program.
L952: On February 3, 2020, the Applicant submitted a request for a fast-track designation. On March 19, 2020, following a review of the request by the FDA, the Applicant was granted a fast-track designation for levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC.
L953: On September 4, 2020, the Applicant submitted a request for a Type C meeting to discuss the use of their topline data from Trial IB1001-201 to support a marketing application. In the meeting held on November 17, 2020, the FDA repeated its position that Trial IB1001-201 was neither adequate nor well-controlled, and therefore would not be sufficient to provide substantial evidence of effectiveness to support a marketing application. The FDA also cautioned the Applicant on their potential plan to seek accelerated approval for levacetylleucine. Meeting minutes were issued on December 2, 2020.
L954: The Applicant subsequently requested a Type C meeting, which was held on May 27, 2021, to discuss the design and endpoints for a new study, Trial IB1001-301, which was proposed as a phase 3, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, crossover study that the Applicant intended to serve as their pivotal trial to support a marketing application. Although the proposed randomized study design for Trial IB1001-301 appeared acceptable, the FDA reminded the Applicant to address some of its previously communicated concerns, such as the clinical outcome measurement instruments for the endpoints, the statistical approach, and the nonclinical and clinical pharmacology development program. The meeting minutes were issued on June 14, 2021.
L955: On September 8, 2021, the Applicant received an orphan drug designation for NALL for NPC.
L956: On November 5, 2021, the Applicant submitted the protocol for Trial IB1001-301, titled "Effects of N-Acetyl-L-Leucine on Niemann-Pick Disease Type C (NPC): A Phase III, Randomized, Placebo-Controlled, Double-Blind, Crossover Study." In an advice letter sent on December 20, 2021, as well as in a subsequent advice letter issued on March 21, 2022, the latter of which was in response to the submission of a revised protocol for IB1001-301 received on January 12, 2022, the FDA provided multiple recommendations including on the measurement approach for the primary endpoint.
L957: On January 9, 2023, in response to a revised protocol for Trial IB1001-201 received on October 19, 2022, the FDA issued an advice letter which reiterated its position that Trial IB1001-201 was not adequately designed to generate interpretable efficacy data.
L958: NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L959: On July 21, 2023, the Applicant requested a premarketing meeting that was granted to discuss the adequacy of their data package to support an NDA for levacetylleucine that was to be submitted via the 505(b)(1) regulatory pathway. A statistical analysis plan was also received on August 7, 2023. During the meeting, which occurred on September 21, 2023, the FDA reminded the Applicant that because they intended their NDA to be supported by a single adequate and well-controlled clinical trial (Trial IB1001-301), additional confirmatory evidence (CE) of the treatment effect would be needed to meet the regulatory standards for a demonstration of substantial evidence of effectiveness; therefore, CE must be provided in their NDA. The FDA recommended that the Applicant request a Type C meeting to further discuss their plan to provide CE. The FDA also noted several missing final reports for nonclinical and clinical pharmacology studies that were needed to support a marketing application. In addition, general guidance was provided for an optimal NDA data package including, but not limited to, clinical outcome assessment evidence, safety, and labeling. Meeting minutes were issued on October 11, 2023.
L960: As a follow-up to the premarketing meeting, the Applicant submitted information regarding their plan to provide CE and nonclinical studies on September 29, 2023. In response, the FDA issued an advice letter on November 24, 2023, providing additional clarifications on CE and nonclinical data.
L961: The Applicant received a rare pediatric disease designation for levacetylleucine for the treatment of NPC on November 18, 2023.
L962: The Applicant submitted NDA 219132 for levacetylleucine, a new molecular entity, for the treatment of NPC via the 505(b)(1) regulatory pathway on January 24, 2024. The Division determined the application would receive a priority review because NPC is a serious condition with no approved treatments.
L963: The proposed proprietary name for levacetylleucine, Aqneursa, was found conditionally acceptable on March 18, 2024. The NDA was filed on March 24, 2024, the midcycle communication with the Applicant was held on May 8, 2024, and the late-cycle meeting with the Applicant was held on August 1, 2024.
L964: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L965: ## 13.1. Summary of Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L966: The nonclinical safety profile of IB1001 (also referred as N-acetyl-L-leucine, NALL, and levacetylleucine) supporting this NDA was evaluated in: (1) repeated dose toxicity studies in rats (up to 6 months) and dogs (up to 9 months); (2) a standard battery of in vitro and in vivo genotoxicity studies; and (3) dose-ranging reproductive and developmental toxicity studies including embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) studies in rats and rabbits. Additional nonclinical studies, including fertility studies in male and female rats, peri- and postnatal developmental study in rats, carcinogenicity studies in transgenic mice (6 months) and rats (2-year bioassay). Juvenile toxicology studies in rats were not included in this submission. Given the imminently life-threatening nature of NPC and the high unmet medical need, these studies will be requested as postmarketing requirements (PMRs).
L967: The following study was previously reviewed under the IND:
L968: ## **Study Number 36243: 6-Week Subchronic Toxicity Study of IB1001 by Repeated Oral Administration to Beagle Dogs**
L969: **Purpose:** Evaluate oral toxicity following daily dosing and reversibility of effects in dogs.
L970: **Design:** Daily oral dosing in Beagle dogs (3M/3F per dose level + 2M/2F in the high dose and control groups) dosed at 0, 225, 750, or 2500 mg/kg/day for 6 weeks followed by a recovery period of 2 weeks. Body weight, feed consumption, electrocardiography (ECG), blood pressure, ophthalmological and auditory examinations, clinical pathology, histopathology, and toxicokinetics (TKs) were evaluated.
L971: **Key Findings:** There were no adverse effects at up to 2500 mg/kg/day. The maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC) levels of levacetylleucine were dose-related after Days 1 and 42 of dosing. The combined male and female C_{max} was 252.8 µg/mL and AUC_{0-\infty} was 598 µg·hr/mL at the no-observed-adverse-effect level (NOAEL) on Day 42.
L972: **Conclusion:** The NOAEL for levacetylleucine was 2500 mg/kg/day in a 6-week oral gavage toxicity study in Beagle dogs. The NOAEL dose in dog provides a 19-fold safety margin based on exposures in humans (31.6 µg·hr/mL) at a daily maximum recommended human dose of 4 g/day.
L973: ## 13.2. Individual Reviews of Studies Submitted With the New Drug Application
L974: ## 13.2.1. Nonclinical Pharmacodynamic Data
L975: Data to support the mechanism of action for levacetylleucine were derived from published literature. The Applicant described data suggesting that in cultured Chinese hamster ovary cells, treatment with levacetylleucine at a concentration of 1mM for 24 hours, was associated with a modest reduction in lysosomal volume, sphingosine, glycosphingolipids (GSLs), and a very modest reduction in stored cholesterol. In NPC patient-derived fibroblasts, treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest reduction in apparent lysosomal volume when cells were cultured for 7 days with 1mM levacetylleucine (Mann 2018). These studies suggest that levacetylleucine has the potential to reduce stored intracellular lipid; however, the mechanism of action for this drug, including the molecular targets by which it elicits these effects, is unclear.
L976: The Applicant also provided published data from an animal disease model. In Npc1^{-/-} mice, pre-symptomatic (from the age of 3 weeks old) treatment with levacetylleucine was also associated with a modest increase in survival and a reduction in markers of neuroinflammation (CD68 staining) in the cerebellum (Section 13.2.2; Kaya et al. 2021). The above changes were not monitored in the clinical trial to evaluate the association of change in magnitude of levels of GSL and neuroinflammation with the dose of levacetylleucine and efficacy. At the Division's request, the Applicant provided raw data obtained by the publication's authors, and a letter of reference granting them the right to use the raw data (submission dated July 3, 2024) and revised right to use raw data certifying that spreadsheets accurately reflect the data contained in publication by Kaya et al. 2021. The figures in sections below are obtained from Kaya et al. 2021 in sequence #0050 (submission dated July 25, 2024). The figures in sections below are obtained from Kaya et al. 2021 with
L977: data for racemic and N-acetyl-D-Leucine, however, the data for only NALL in comparison with appropriate controls is described.
L978: ## In Vitro Mechanism of Action
L979: ## Study of NALL Treatment on Lysosomal Volume and Accumulation of Sphingosines In Vitro
L980: The effects of NALL on lysosomal volume and levels of sphingosine, GSLs, and free cholesterol were measured in cultured wildtype and NPC1-deficient (Npc1^{-/-}) Chinese hamster ovary cells. Treatment of Npc1^{-/-} Chinese hamster ovary cells with 1mM NALL reduced lysosomal volume compared to untreated cells after 24 hours (16%; p=0.0177) and 72 hours of treatment (22.8%; p=0.0036) (Figure 14 panel A). Levacetylleucine reduced sphingosine levels by 29% compared to untreated cells (p&lt;0.0001). Additionally, NALL significantly reduced cholesterol content by 13% compared to untreated cells (p=0.0263) (Figure 14 panel B).

L987: ## Study of NALL Treatment on Lysosomal Volume in Fibroblasts From Patients With NPC
L988: The effect of 1mM levacetylleucine on fibroblasts from patients with NPC with severe clinical presentation was evaluated by analyzing change in LysoTracker fluorescence associated with change in lysosomal volume. The treatment of fibroblasts from patients with NPC with levacetylleucine for 7 days modestly reduced the LysoTracker fluorescence, suggesting that the drug reduced lysosomal volume compared to untreated fibroblasts from patients with NPC. There was no numerical difference in the magnitude of the effect when cells were treated with levacetylleucine versus when they were treated with N-acetyl-DL-leucine.

L992: ## In Vivo Mechanism of Action
L993: ## Study of NALL Treatment on Lipid Storage in Brain in *Npc1-/-* Mice
L994: The effect of administering levacetylleucine in pre-symptomatic *Npc1-/-* mice beginning at the age of 3 weeks old, was evaluated by measuring lipid storage in brain. The *Npc1-/-* mice exhibited an increase in total GSL levels in forebrain and cerebellum compared to wild type mice at 59 days (approximately 8.5 weeks) of age (Figure 16 panels A and B; Kaya et al. 2021). Treatment of *Npc1-/-* mice with levacetylleucine at 0.1 mg/kg/day modestly reduced (not significantly) the total GSL levels compared to untreated *Npc1-/-* mice. The reduction in certain GSLs was more pronounced with reduction in GM1a (20.1%; p=0.0018) and GM2 (19.6%; p=0.0222) in the forebrain and the reduction of GA2 (23.8%; p=0.0042) and GA1 (23.5%; p=0.0254) in cerebellum.

L1003: ## Study of NALL Treatment on Cerebellar Ataxia Motor Skills and Long-Term Neuroprotective Effect in Pre-Symptomatic *Npc1*-/- Mice.
L1004: The Applicant states that the lifespan of *Npc1*-/- mice is about 10 to 12 weeks with onset of symptoms, such as gait abnormalities, tremor, and weight loss beginning at between the ages of 6 and 7 weeks old. The Applicant states that at 9 weeks of age, untreated *Npc1*-/- mice show statistically significant ataxia that presents as pronounced sigmoid gait compared to wildtype *Npc1*-/- mice (p&lt;0.0001). Ataxia was determined by measuring lateral displacement from a straight trajectory in an automated gait analysis system (Figure 17 panels A and B). Kaya et al. 2021 reported that treatment of *Npc1*-/- mice with 100 mg/kg/day of levacetylleucine for 1 week beginning at the age of 8 weeks old showed significant reduction in ataxia (p&lt;0.0001) compared to untreated *Npc1*-/- mice. In mice treated pre-symptomatically from weaning (i.e., at the age of 3 weeks old) and assessed at the age of 9 weeks old (i.e., after 6 weeks of treatment), levacetylleucine also significantly reduced the magnitude of ataxia compared to that of the untreated *Npc1*-/- mice (p&lt;0.0001). The effect on motor function was more prominent when animals were treated pre-symptomatically than when
L1005: treatment was initiated in Npc1^{-/-} mice after symptoms had started (p&lt;0.003) (Figure 17 panels A and B).
L1006: **Figure 17. Gait Analysis of Mice With NALL Treatment Initiated in the Symptomatic or Pre-Symptomatic Period Compared With Untreated Npc1^{-/-} Mice and Wildtype Controls**

L1011: ## Study of Pre-Symptomatic Treatment With NALL on Gait Abnormalities, Motor Function, and Lifespan (“Survival”) of Npc1^{-/-} Mice
L1012: The effects of levacetylleucine on motor dysfunction during ambulation were measured in Npc1^{-/-} mice using the Rotarod, which measures coordination and balance, and using the CatWalk gait analysis, which measures paw placement abnormalities. As shown in Figure 18, treatment of pre-symptomatic Npc1^{-/-} mice had a significant improvement in hind paw duty cycle (Figure 18 panel A) and hind paw stand mean (Figure 18 panel B) in CatWalk test compared to untreated Npc1^{-/-} mice (Panel A). Treatment of Npc1^{-/-} mice with 0.1 g/kg/day levacetylleucine also led to significant functional improvements in hind paw duty cycle (Figure 18 panel A) and hind paw stand mean (Figure 18 panel B) in mice treated after onset of symptoms compared to untreated Npc1^{-/-} mice. The duty cycle is the percentage of the step cycle spent to the stand phase versus the swing phase (i.e., duty cycle % = stand phase/step cycle) (Haulcomb et al. 2017).
L1013: Treatment with levacetylleucine at 0.1 g/kg/day in pre-symptomatic Npc1^{-/-} mice beginning at the age of 3 weeks old improved motor function as measured as time spent on the accelerating Rotarod (starting from 1 rpm to 10 rpm, accelerating by one rpm every 30 seconds) compared to untreated Npc1^{-/-} mice (Figure 18 panel C). Additionally, pre-symptomatic treatment (treatment initiated at weaning) with levacetylleucine of Npc1^{-/-} mice increased lifespan (+9.1%) compared to untreated Npc1^{-/-} mice (Figure 18 panel D).

L1020: A. Duty cycle of Npc1^{-/-} mice treated pre-symptomatically with N-acetyl-L-leucine (ALL) compared with untreated (UT) wildtype and Npc1^{-/-} controls. Front and hind duty cycle measurements. Mean \pmSD, **p&lt;0.032, ***p&lt;0.0001 (2-way ANOVA).
L1021: B. Front and hind stand mean measurements of Npc1^{-/-} mice treated pre-symptomatically with N-acetyl-L-leucine (ALL) compared with untreated (UT) wildtype and Npc1^{-/-} controls. Mean \pmSD, * p&lt;0.026, **p&lt;0.003 (2-way ANOVA).
L1022: C. Early-stage N-acetyl-L-leucine treatment motor performance measurements. Mean \pmSD, **p&lt;0.009, ***p&lt;0.0001 (one-way ANOVA).
L1023: D. Life expectancy percentages of Npc1^{-/-} mice treated pre-symptomatically with N-acetyl-L-leucine (ALL) compared with untreated (UT) Npc1^{-/-} mice. Mean \pmSD, * p&lt;0.046 (Gehan-Breslow-Wilcoxon test).
L1024: Abbreviations: ADL = acetyl-d-leucine; ADLL = Acetyl-DL-leucine; ALL = acetyl-L-leucine; ANOVA = analysis of variance; SD = standard deviation; UT = untreated
L1025: ## Study of Neuroinflammation in the Brain of Npc1^{-/-} Mice
L1026: The Applicant states that neuroinflammation in the brain is a characteristic of NPC, and that this manifests as progressive loss of cerebellar Purkinje cells in Npc1^{-/-} mice, progressing from the anterior to posterior lobes, and is accompanied by microglial activation. Npc1^{-/-} mice treated with 0.1 g/kg/day levacetylleucine in the diet from the age of 3 weeks old show a reduction in the frequency of CD68 positive (a marker of microglial activation) cells at 59 days (about 8 weeks) old compared to untreated Npc1^{-/-} mice (20%; p=0.077). The authors state that this suggests a slowing of disease progression by levacetylleucine (Figure 19 panels A and B).
L1027: **Figure 19. Effect of NALL on Activated Microglia in Npc1^{-/-} Mice at the Age of 59 Days**
L1028: ## B.
L1029: Source: (Kaya et al. 2021)
L1030: N-acetyl-L-leucine (=ALL) was administered to treated *Npc1*-/- mice in the diet (0.1 g/kg/day) from the age of 3 weeks.
L1031: A. Cerebellum stained with calbindin-b (Purkinje cells, red) or CD68 (activated microglia, yellow).

L1033: The Applicant cited additional publications that describe other potential modes of action whereby NALL may improve aspects of cellular function in the context of neurodegenerative diseases; however, the data were either not specific to NPC or were of uncertain relevance to the proposed context of use.
L1034: ## 13.2.2. Safety Pharmacology
L1035: The Applicant evaluated safety pharmacology endpoints in good laboratory practice (GLP)-compliant stand-alone studies to evaluate effects on the central nervous system, cardiovascular system, and respiratory system.
L1036: ## 13.2.2.1. Central Nervous System
L1037: **Study Title:** Neuropsychological screening in rats according to Irwin following oral administration of IB1001 (Study 38192) (GLP compliant)
L1038: **Methods:** Effects of levacetylleucine were evaluated in female rats (8 in each group) following administration of a single oral dose of 250, 800, or 2500 mg/kg (dose volume of 10 mL/kg). Effects were evaluated on the following 40 neuropsychological parameters at 15-, 30-, 60- and 120-minute postdose timepoints.

L1042: **Results:** At oral doses of 250, 800, or 2500 mg/kg, levacetylleucine had no adverse effects on any of the neuropharmacological parameters compared to controls. The oral NOAEL of levacetylleucine on neuropharmacological parameters was 2500 mg/kg; however, the design of the study was not adequately justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (females), the selection of the high dose). Based on the single-dose C_{max} exposure measured in a 26-week study (Study 36242) in which rats were dosed at 2500 mg/kg, the safety multiple at the NOAEL dose of 2500 mg/kg is 5- to 10-fold when compared to the respective steady-state C_{max} levels in humans.
L1043: ## 13.2.2.2. Cardiovascular System
L1044: **Study Title:** IB1001: Telemetric Evaluation of Cardiovascular Effects in the Conscious Beagle Dog (Oral Gavage Administration) (Study 8440125) (GLP compliant)
L1045: **Methods:** Effects of single dose of levacetylleucine were evaluated when administered by oral gavage in telemetered beagle dogs at doses of 100, 200 or 600 mg/kg. This study was conducted in four surgically instrumented male Beagle dogs using a Latin Square crossover design, in which each dog received all dose levels in a randomized manner. Doses were separated by a washout period of at least 6 days (which is greater than the 10 half-lives [t_{1/2}] of &lt;2 hours). Endpoints evaluated included arterial blood pressure (systolic, diastolic, and derived mean), heart rate, body temperature, ECG lead II intervals (PR interval, QRS interval, QT, and heart rate corrected QT) and waveform morphology. The vehicle was NaCl 0.9%, w/v, pH 8.0. The telemetric data were collected 120 min before dosing through 24 hours post dose. The measured data were averaged at hourly intervals and compared to controls. Clinical and behavioral signs were observed as 1-, 2- and 4-hours post dose. The design of the study was not adequately justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (males), the selection of the high dose).
L1046: **Results:** Levacetylleucine, at oral doses of 100, 200 or 600 mg/kg, caused no adverse clinical or behavioral signs, or effects on body temperature and cardiovascular parameters (blood pressure [systolic, diastolic, and mean], heart rate, ECG intervals [PR interval, QRS interval, QT, and heart rate corrected QT], and ECG waveform morphology) compared to vehicle control. The oral NOAEL of levacetylleucine for cardiovascular effect was 600 mg/kg.
L1047: Based on the steady state C_{max} exposure (M 61.1 µg/mL/F 83.9 µg/mL) measured in a 39-week study (Study 8422660) in which dogs were dosed at 600 mg/kg, the safety multiple at the NOAEL dose is 8- to 12-fold when compared to the respective steady-state C_{max} (7.4 µg/mL) in humans. The dose formulation analysis indicated that the formulations met prespecified acceptance criteria, as concentrations ranged between 95.9% and 105.0% of the nominal concentrations and within the acceptable range of 90 to 110%.
L1048: NDA 219132
Aqneursa (levacetylleucine)
L1049: **Study Title:** IB1001: Effect on human ether-a-go-go-related gene (hERG) Tail Currents Recorded From Stably Transfected 293 human embryonic kidney (HEK293) Cells (Study A2592) (GLP compliant)
L1050: **Methods:** Levacetylleucine was tested for its effects on the hERG outward tail currents recorded in HEK293 cells stably transfected with cDNA encoding this cardiac potassium channel. The test was performed to evaluate the potential for QT-prolongation by levacetylleucine. The concentrations tested were at the close to the limit of solubility (concentration of 1000\muM/525 \muM [nominal/actual concentration determined by analysis of dose formulations after completion of the study]) E-4031 (a methane sulfonanilide class III antiarrhythmic drug) was used as positive control.
L1051: **Results:** At the highest feasible concentration of 525\muM, levacetylleucine inhibited hERG tail currents by less than 30%, therefore, half-maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) could not be determined, as the concentration response curve was limited by solubility. 525\muM (91 \mug/mL) provide a more than 12-fold safety margin when compared to the mean, steady-state C_{max} level in human plasma (7.4 \mug/mL). The results indicate that levacetylleucine is not likely to inhibit the hERG channel when administered at the intended doses.
L1052: ## 13.2.2.3. Respiratory System
L1053: **Study Title:** Examination of Respiratory Parameters in Rats Following Oral Administration of IB1001 Employing Whole Body Plethysmography (Study 38191) (GLP)
L1054: **Methods:** Levacetylleucine was tested at single oral doses of 250, 800 and 2500 mg/kg, administered by oral gavage at dose volume of 10 mL/kg in female rats (8 in each group). Respiratory endpoints (respiratory rate, tidal volume, minute volume, peak inspiratory flow, peak expiratory flow, inspiratory time, expiratory time, and the airway resistance index) were measured by whole-body plethysmography for 4 hours postdose (baseline was measured predose for 1 hour). The vehicle control was NaCl 0.9 %, w/v, pH 8.0 and Carbamyl-\beta-methylcholine chloride (bethanechol) at 30 mg (subcutaneous) was used as positive control. Respiratory parameters were assessed at intervals of 10 minutes for the first hour (10 minutes to 60 minutes) and intervals of 30 minutes postdose for the remainder of the experiment (1 hour to 4 hours).
L1055: **Results:** Levacetylleucine, at oral doses of 250, 800 and 2500 mg/kg, did not affect respiratory parameters (respiratory rate, tidal volume, minute volume, peak inspiratory flow, peak expiratory flow, inspiratory time, expiratory time, and the airway resistance index) in female rats following administration of a single dose. The oral NOAEL of levacetylleucine on respiratory parameters was 2500 mg/kg. Based on the single-dose C_{max} exposure measured in a 26-week study (Study 36242) in which rats were dosed at 2500 mg/kg, the safety multiple at the NOAEL dose of 2500 mg/kg is 5- to 10-fold when compared to the respective steady-state C_{max} in humans (Table 62); however, the design of the study was not adequately justified (e.g., the use of a single sex (females), the selection of the high dose).

L1062: ## **Observations and Results**
L1063: ## **Mortality**
L1064: None.
L1065: ## **Clinical Signs**
L1066: Salivation and soft feces were observed in both males and females at 2500 mg/kg/day.
L1067: ## **Body Weights**
L1068: Decreased body weight (-6%) was noted in males at 2500 mg/kg/day. This was subsided at the end of the 4-week recovery period. No body weight effect was noted in females.
L1069: ## Feed Consumption
L1070: Decreased food consumption (-11%) was noted in females at 2500 mg/kg/day, without correlation to decreased body weight (not considered adverse). This was subsided at the end of the 4-week recovery period. No effect was noted in males.
L1071: In addition, increased drinking water consumption was noted in both males and females. This was also subsided at the end of the 4-week recovery period.

L1108: ## Toxicokinetics
L1109: - The C_{max} and AUC revealed a roughly linear and dose-related exposure of the animals to IB1001 after the oral administration on Day 1 and a non-linear exposure on Days 91 and 176/178.
- The calculated plasma half-lives (t_{1/2}) were 0.25 to 4 hours ranged from 0.80 to 4.22 hours in males and from 0.62 to 6.71 hours in females.
- Plateau levels were still observed at 4 hours, in particular for the intermediate dose (800 mg/kg/day) and high dose (2500 mg/kg/day).
- There was no gender difference in exposure at the low and intermediate dose level groups. However, the C_{max} of the high dosed females on Days 91 and 176 were approx. 2-fold higher than the C_{max} of the high dosed males.
- The C_{max} and AUC of the high dose level group were slightly lower on Day 91 (males and females) and on Day 178 (males only) compared to the values of Day 1.
- No accumulation with time was noted.

L1112: In the 26-week toxicity study, administration of levacetylleucine (0, 250, 800, and 2500 mg/kg/day) by oral gavage in rats with a 4-week treatment-free recovery period produced no adverse effects at doses of up to 800 mg/kg/day; thus, the NOAEL is 800 mg/kg/day. At 2500 mg/kg/day test article related adverse effects include decreased body weight (males only), changes of clinical chemistry parameters (males and females), changes of urine parameters (females only), increased kidney weights (males and females), histopathological changes in the kidney (males only). Exposure (AUC0-\infty) in rats dosed at 800 mg/kg provide a safety margin of 2- to 3-fold when compared to the respective steady-state levels in humans (Table 62).
L1113: ## 6.2.2 Study Title: IB1001: 39 week oral (gavage) administration toxicity study in the dog followed by a 4 week recovery period
L1114: Study no.:	8422660

L1145: ## Toxicokinetics
L1146: - Sex differences in IB1001 mean C_{max} and AUC_{0-24} values were less than 2-fold.
- Exposure increased with the increase in dose level from 100 to 600 mg/kg/day. The increases in IB1001 mean C_{max} and AUC_{0-24} values were generally dose proportional from 100 to 200 mg/kg/day and less than dose proportional from 200 to 600 mg/kg/day.
- No notable accumulation of IB1001 was observed after multiple doses in dogs.

L1150: Levacetylleucine (0, 100, 200, and 600 mg/kg/day) administered by oral gavage in dogs in a 39-week toxicity study followed by a 4-week treatment-free period was without adverse effects with NOAEL of 600 mg/kg/day. Non-adverse findings were limited to liquid feces observed at 600 mg/kg/day. Exposure (AUC_{0-24h}) in dogs dosed at 600 mg/kg provides safety margins of 6- to 9-fold when compared to the respective steady-state levels in humans (Table 62).
L1151: ## 13.2.4. Genetic Toxicology
L1152: Levacetylleucine was non-mutagenic in vitro (in bacterial cells) and non-clastogenic in vitro (in cultured human peripheral lymphocytes) and in vivo (in bone marrow cells of the CD rats).

L1163: ## Results
L1164: Levacetylleucine was not mutagenic in *Salmonella* Typhimurium strains TA98, TA100, TA102, TA1535, or TA1537 at exposures between 10 to 5000 µg/plate in presence or absence of S9 when tested by plate incorporation and preincubation methods. The results
L1165: indicate concentrations at or above 2500 µg/plate resulted in reduced background colonies in some or all strains (Table 51).
L1166: Levacetylleucine did not produce greater than 2-fold increase in revertant colonies in *Salmonella* Typhimurium strains TA98, TA100, TA1535 and TA1537 and 1.5-fold increase in revertant colonies for TA102 or concentration-related increase in revertant colonies compared to vehicle control (Table 51). Positive controls produced greater than 2-fold increases in all strains in the presence or absence of S9 when tested by plate incorporation and preincubation methods.
L1167: This study included the levacetylleucine impurities, (b) (4)

L1180: ## Results
L1181: Levacetylleucine, at up to 2000 µg/mL, was evaluated to be non-genotoxic because it did not produce chromosomal, or chromatid damage in cultured human lymphocytes in the presence (4-hour incubation) or absence (4- and 24-hour incubations) of S9 compared to vehicle control (Table 53). Mitomycin positive control produced significant increases in chromosomal aberrations compared to vehicle control.
L1182: This study included the levacetylleucine impurities, (b) (4) due to their reported presence at designated levels.

L1197: ## 13.2.5. Carcinogenicity
L1198: Rodent carcinogenicity studies have not been conducted with levacetylleucine. PMRs for carcinogenicity will be issued because levacetylleucine is likely to be administered for a lifetime beginning in infancy or early childhood.
L1199: ## 13.2.6. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L1200: ## 13.2.6.1. Fertility and Early Embryonic Development
L1201: A fertility and early embryonic development study in rats has not been conducted with levacetylleucine. PMRs for evaluating effects of levacetylleucine on fertility and early embryonic development will be issued because there are adult-onset forms of NPC, and treatment with levacetylleucine for early onset NPC may continue into reproductive age; therefore, some patients of reproductive age are likely to be exposed. Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid and appears to have widespread distribution; therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected.
L1202: ## 13.2.6.2. Embryonic and Fetal Development
L1203: Dose-ranging embryonic and fetal development studies in rats and rabbits have been conducted with levacetylleucine. The data from the Applicant's dose-ranging studies indicate levacetylleucine is potentially fetotoxic and causes effects on embryofetal development in rats and rabbits, as described below.
L1204: The Applicant conducted a 10-day oral dose-range finding study in non-pregnant rabbits at 1250 or 2500 mg/kg/day in two groups of three female rabbits New Zealand White (HsdHra:(NZW)SPF) to determine tolerability of levacetylleucine. A decrease in food
L1205: consumption and macroscopic observations of brown fluid in the cecum and urinary bladder (single female) were observed at 2500 mg/kg/day. The results of the dose-range finding study in non-pregnant rabbits were used to select doses of 675, 1250 and 2500 mg/kg/day for the main study.
L1206: ## 13.2.6.2.1. Study Title: Embryonic and Fetal Development Study in Rats (Terminal Sacrifice; Study 8519317)

L1244: ## **Conclusion**
L1245: In conclusion, in phase 1, daily oral administration via gavage from GD 6 to 17, IB1001 at up to 1000 mg/kg/day was maternally tolerated in rats. Levacetylleucine appeared to be fetotoxic at doses 600 mg/kg/day and greater with increase in resorptions and post-implantation loss. Levacetylleucine is considered to cause effects on embryofetal development at doses 600 mg/kg/day and greater due to observation of visceral and skeletal malformation in thoracic arch of the vertebra (fused thoracic arch, misaligned thoracic arch, hemicentric thoracic centrum, and misaligned thoracic centrum), vertebral column (scoliosis), and ribs (absent costal cartilage and short rib).

L1254: ## **Mortality**
L1255: There was no treatment-related mortality in phase 1. A single 1250 mg/kg/day female (Animal R0201) died following dosing on GD 13. The death was considered a gavage error based on the observed presence of red discoloration and perforation of the lung lobes and red/tan contents in the trachea.
L1256: ## **Clinical Signs**
L1257: Four of six rabbits administered 2500 mg/kg/day appeared thin at one or two instances and this was considered incidental. No dose related clinical signs were observed.
L1258: ## **Body Weight**
L1259: Body weight loss and decreased body weight gain were observed primarily around GD 22; however, the effect was not dose dependent (Figure 21).

L1276: ## Cesarean Section Data
L1277: ## *Reproductive Assessment*
L1278: All phase 1 females were pregnant. On GD 13, one 1250 mg/kg/day female (Animal R0201) died following dosing but was considered due to gavage error and not related to test article effect.
L1279: In all levacetylleucine-treated groups, mean number of live fetuses per litter was lower and reached statistical significance in 675 mg/kg/day and 2500 mg/kg/day groups. However, reduction in live fetuses in low dose group is likely due to fewer implantations and not test article-related effects. The mean number of early resorptions, mean number of total resorptions, mean resorption rate, and mean post-implantation loss were higher in 2500 mg/kg/day than controls (Table 60).
L1280: Mean gravid uterine weight was lower than control in all levacetylleucine-treated groups and reached statistical significance in the 2500 mg/kg/day (-39.9%). The decrease in gravid uterine weight correlated with increase in post-implantation loss in this group.
L1281: ## F1 Offspring
L1282: ## Terminal Observations
L1283: ## Fetal Weights
L1284: Mean fetal weights (males, females, and the sexes combined) were lower than controls, although not statistically significant, in a dose-responsive manner in mid (1250 mg/kg/day) and high (2500 mg/kg/day) dose groups (Table 60).
L1285: ## Fetal Malformations/Variations (External, Visceral, Skeletal)
L1286: Fetal external malformations observed in 2500 mg/kg/day group included open eyes (two of six litters), partially open eyes (three of six litters), and hyperflexion of the limbs (two of six litters). Other external malformations were of low occurrence or lacked dose responsiveness (Table 60).
L1287: The fetal visceral developmental variation of absent accessory lung lobe was observed in four of six litters (67%) at the 2500 mg/kg/day dose level (Table 60).
L1288: Fetal skeletal malformations observed in 2500 mg/kg/day included misshapen maxilla (two of six litters), misshapen premaxilla (two of six litters, 33%), and small interparietal bone (one of six litters, 17%). Skeletal developmental variations indicative of delayed ossification (e.g., incomplete ossification of frontal bone, palatine, and sternebrae) were observed in the 1250 or 2500 mg/kg/day. The above findings in the 2500 mg/kg/day correlated with lower fetal body weights (Table 60).

L1296: ## Conclusion
L1297: In conclusion, in phase 1 animals, daily oral administration of IB1001 via gavage from GD 6 to 19 at up to 2500 mg/kg/day was maternally tolerated with decreases in food consumption at the highest dose. Levacetylleucine appeared to be fetotoxic at doses of \ge 1250 mg/kg/day with a dose-related increase in resorptions and post-implantation loss that corresponded with a decrease in mean maternal weight, and a slight decrease in the mean number of live fetuses at doses greater than 675 mg/kg/day. There was also a decrease in mean fetal body weight.
L1298: Levacetylleucine induced fetal malformations in dams treated at doses of \ge 1250 mg/kg/day. Findings included fetal external malformations of open/partially open eyes and hyperflexion of the limbs, fetal skeletal malformations of small interparietal bone and misshapen maxilla and/or premaxilla. The adverse embryofetal findings observed at doses \ge 1250 mg/kg/day occurred in the absence of overt toxicity to the dam; therefore, N-acetyl-L-leucine is considered a selective developmental toxicant.
L1299: ## 13.2.6.3. Prenatal and Postnatal Development
L1300: The prenatal and postnatal development study in rats has not been conducted with levacetylleucine. PMRs for evaluating effects of levacetylleucine on pre- and postnatal development will be issued because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and treatment with levacetylleucine may range from infancy into late adulthood. Levacetylleucine was found to cause increases in post-implantation loss and malformation in skeletal bones in a dose-range finding (DRF) EFD study in rabbits. Leucine, a metabolite of levacetylleucine, injection as a single 0.2 mL of a 1% solution in the amnionic sac of a 9-day-old embryo of chicken is reported to cause morphological anomalies of the legs (including inward flexion of the toes, leg extension or flexion at hip joint), defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck and depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967). Therefore, exposure to levacetylleucine has potential to cause adverse effects on growth and development (neurological, physical, and sexual) from prenatal and into postnatal stage of life and requires investigation. Additionally, levacetylleucine is a modified amino acid and appears to have widespread distribution; therefore, placental and lactational transfer are expected.
L1301: ## 13.2.6.4. Juvenile Animal Studies
L1302: A DRF juvenile toxicity study was conducted in postnatal day (PND) 7-PND 28 Wistar Han rats (six animals/sex/group) with levacetylleucine at doses 300, 600, 1000 mg/kg administered once daily (QD) by oral gavage. The animal was observed for survival, clinical and post dose observations, body weights and food consumption. At termination animals were observed for macroscopic changes. A dose of 1000 mg/kg/day was found to be tolerated without significant toxicological findings in any of the above parameters.
L1303: (b) (4)
L1304: PMRs for evaluating toxicity of levacetylleucine on juvenile animals will be issued because onset of NPC ranges from infancy into adulthood, and treatment with levacetylleucine may range from infancy into adulthood. In addition, nonclinical studies available at the times of NDA submission used doses that lacked an adequate justification, as they were not clearly based on achieving a maximum tolerated dose, maximum feasible dose, or saturation of exposure with dose, nor did they achieve a sufficient safety margin relative to the clinical dose. Further, the rat and rabbit EFD studies indicate levacetylleucine crossed placenta and modulated growth of bones, and it is uncertain if levacetylleucine will modulate the growth of bones in juvenile animals. Considering leucine causes defects in ossification of the skull, torsion of the neck and depression of the brain waves (Bergstrom et al. 1967), levacetylleucine has potential to influence growth, neurological development (learning and memory), sexual development, and general health. Hence, there is need for a juvenile toxicity study to determine the toxic effects of levacetylleucine on juvenile animals and inform patients of potential risks of medication.
